### Phase 12a — Minimal Cloud Deploy (EC2 + docker-compose) *(1.5–2 days)*

**Pulled forward from Phase 12 on 2026-08-29 (see the reorder note in the checklist section). Runs after Phase 10a, before Phase 10b.** The goal is narrow: **v1 genuinely deployed** — a public HTTPS API and UI on AWS, auth on, staying up 24/7 with no laptop in the loop — so that "in production" stops being notional before the retrain-automation work (10b/10c). It is the deploy slice of Phase 12 only; the rest stays in **Phase 12b**.

**Deployment target: a single EC2 `t3.small` running docker-compose, plus RDS `db.t3.micro` for state.** Originally scoped as both AWS-free-tier for 12 months at $0/mo running cost — **corrected 2026-09-16: this account's Billing → Free Tier usage page confirms no classic per-service free-tier quotas are active** (no EC2/RDS/EBS/EIP line items, only the perpetual always-free tiers for unrelated services), so the actual running cost is **~$36–38/mo** at standard `us-east-1` rates (`t3.small` ~$15.18 + 20 GB gp3 root ~$1.60 + `db.t3.micro` ~$12.41 + 20 GB RDS storage ~$2.30 + Elastic IP ~$3.65 + S3/ECR/transfer ~$1–2), metered against the account's shared, finite Free Plan credit balance (Group 0) rather than free. **Bumped from `t3.micro` to `t3.small` on 2026-09-18** — a reviewer-facing MLflow UI was added to Group 9's scope, and the original `t3.micro` RAM budget below had already found no room for it; see that group's checklist for the real dollar delta (~+$7.59/mo) this decision carries. See Group 0's cost-minimization bullet for how to reduce this. App Runner and ECS-Fargate have no free tier either (~$9/mo per always-on task; `api` + `ui` + a tracking server ≈ $20–27/mo **compute-only** — add RDS (~$12.41) + S3/ECR (~$1–2) on top, same as the EC2 total already does, to reach the apples-to-apples ~$34–41/mo cited at this plan's other App Runner comparisons, e.g. line 249/642's ~$34/mo) — priced out against the EC2 total above on that same all-in basis, they cost *more*, not less, so this project does not build a migration to either: they're a **documented recommendation in `README.md`** for if this ever needed real production traffic, not a Phase 12b deliverable. A single VM with docker-compose is a legitimate production pattern for a service that does not need to scale — what it lacks (autoscaling, multi-AZ, zero-downtime deploys, host-patching handled for you) is a separate *resilience* axis, not "production vs. not". That framing is documented — see the doc-ripple bullet below.

**What this achieves:** `serving/app.py` runs as a container on an always-on EC2 instance, reachable at `https://<domain>`, resolving `models:/telco-churn-pipeline@champion` at boot **directly against RDS + S3** — `api`'s own model resolution never depends on a running MLflow server. A separate MLflow server container *does* run on the same box (added 2026-09-18, Group 9) purely so a reviewer can browse the experiments themselves at `https://<domain>/mlflow`, without needing AWS credentials or a laptop-side SSM tunnel — see that group's RAM/cost note. The Streamlit UI runs alongside. A request with no `X-API-Key` is refused. The instance survives reboot, the containers auto-restart, and the developer's laptop is irrelevant to uptime.

> **📌 This rides entirely on Phase 7's promotion — it needs nothing from Phase 10b or 10c.** v1 is already tagged `promotion_status: promoted` with `@champion` pointing at it, minted through the current three-state lifecycle (`pending | promoted | rejected`) in Phase 7's manual cycle. 12a is **infrastructure only**: it stands up EC2 / RDS / S3 / ECR and points the serving container at them. Nothing re-runs the promotion path, so the four-state lifecycle split (`approved`/`staged`), `approve_challenger`/`promote_approved`, `guardrails.py`, and `canary_rollout.py` are all irrelevant here — they exist for promoting a *new challenger* through the progressive-deployment path, which is 10b/10c. The serving layer (`resolve_champion_model`, Phase 9) resolves the `@champion` *alias* and never reads `promotion_status` tags at all, so it is unaffected by 10c's vocabulary change.

**Deliverables — infrastructure (IaC under `infra/`, Terraform or CDK):**
- **EC2 `t3.micro`** (Amazon Linux 2023), public subnet, security group opening 22 (SSH, your IP only), 80, 443. User-data installs Docker + the compose plugin, `systemctl enable docker`, and a small systemd unit that runs `docker compose up -d` from `/opt/telco-churn/` on boot; containers also carry `restart: unless-stopped`. **Superseded by what was actually built (see `docs/PHASE_12A_TODO.md` Groups 3/8/9) — this bullet is the original plan, kept for history, not the live spec:** the real instance is **`t3.small`** (Ubuntu 24.04 LTS, not Amazon Linux — RAM-bumped 2026-09-18 to fit the reviewer-facing MLflow container), with **no inbound 22 at all** — shell access is SSM Session Manager only, keyless, not an IP-restricted SSH rule.
- **RDS `db.t3.micro`** Postgres (single-AZ, 20 GB gp3), private subnet, reachable only from the EC2 security group. Holds **both** the MLflow backend store (`mlflow` DB) and the application DB. Schema via `alembic upgrade head` (Alembic, 10a-i), run once from the box.
- **S3 bucket `telco-churn-mlflow`** — MLflow artifact store; bucket policy allows only the EC2 instance role. The `telco-churn-data` DVC remote stays a Phase 12b item.
- **ECR repositories** for `api` and `ui` (built from `docker/api/Dockerfile` / `docker/ui/Dockerfile`, from `uv.lock`). **Lifecycle policy:** expire untagged after 7 days, keep the last 5 tagged.
- **EC2 instance IAM role** — least-privilege: `ecr:GetAuthorizationToken` + pull on the two repos, `s3:GetObject`/`PutObject` on the bucket, `ssm:GetParameter` on `/telco-churn/*`. No long-lived keys anywhere.
- **SSM Parameter Store** (not Secrets Manager — SSM standard params are free) for `API_KEY` and the RDS connection string, read into the containers at compose-up.
- **CloudWatch agent** on the box shipping container logs + basic host metrics (free tier: 10 metrics, 5 GB logs). `LOG_FORMAT=json` in the container env so Logs Insights can query structured fields.
- **Billing alarms at $100/$140 plus an AWS Budgets budget at the same thresholds** — **corrected 2026-09-16, superseding an earlier "$1 alarm + 12-month free-tier expiry" draft of this bullet.** That framing assumed genuine free-tier eligibility (cost starts at $0, then rises after 12 months); Group 0's account-status check found the opposite — this account has no classic free-tier quotas active, so the ~$36–38/mo cost (line 607's itemized figure, post Group 9's `t3.small` bump) applies from the moment these resources exist, and a $1 alarm would fire immediately and teach nothing. See Group 0 for the real thresholds (scaled to the account's actual $155.32 shared credit balance) and the 2027-01-16 credit-cutoff reminder, which replaces the "12-month expiry" concept for this account.

**Deliverables — serving stack (`compose.prod.yml` on the box):**
- **`api`** container, 24/7. `MLFLOW_TRACKING_URI=postgresql://…@<rds-endpoint>/mlflow` — a **direct DB URI, no MLflow server needed for `api`'s own resolution**; the client resolves runs/registry straight against RDS, artifacts from S3. `auth.enabled=true` via Hydra override, `API_KEY` from SSM. Health check `/ready` (resolves Phase 9's open one-check mapping — model-loaded is what should gate traffic).
- **`ui`** container (Streamlit). Add to the 24/7 compose. **Includes the `ui/streamlit_app.py::_api_request` `X-API-Key` header fix** — `headers={"X-API-Key": os.environ["API_KEY"], **kwargs.pop("headers", {})}` when the env var is set (a no-op locally) — or the UI 401s against its own API once auth is on.
- **`caddy`** container — automatic Let's Encrypt HTTPS terminating TLS for `api`, `ui`, and `mlflow`. Needs a domain: a cheap registered one or a free subdomain (DuckDNS / a subdomain you own). ~15 MB.
- **`mlflow` container — reviewer-facing MLflow UI, scoped in 2026-09-18 (reverses this bullet's earlier "no MLflow server container" decision).** Same `docker/mlflow/Dockerfile` local dev already uses (plus a `boto3` addition — the official MLflow image doesn't bundle it, same gap Group 6.5 found and fixed for the training dependency tree), pointed at RDS+S3 instead of local Postgres, `--static-prefix /mlflow` so it shares the box's one hostname/cert rather than needing a second DNS record, reachable at `https://<domain>/mlflow` behind Caddy `basic_auth` (MLflow's UI has no API-key concept like `api`'s `X-API-Key`, so this is the equivalent control). A published demo login in `README.md`, same justification as the API demo key: the dataset is public/synthetic, so the point of auth is to demonstrate the control, not protect anything sensitive. This is what actually lets a reviewer browse `03a`/`03b`'s live experiment evidence themselves, without AWS credentials or an SSM tunnel of their own.

> **📌 RAM budget — the addition above is why the instance moved from `t3.micro` (1 GB) to `t3.small` (2 GB), not a `t3.micro` afterthought.** `api` (model loaded) ~300–500 MB, `ui` ~150 MB, `caddy` ~15 MB, CloudWatch agent ~50 MB, Grafana Alloy ~30–100 MB (Group 12), OS ~150 MB, **`mlflow` server ~150–250 MB** → **~880–1,200 MB total**, which the original `t3.micro` budget explicitly could not absorb (that estimate, without `mlflow`, was already at ~730–950 MB of the 1 GB available). `t3.small` gives real headroom instead of a second tight-fit gamble. Real recurring cost impact: ~+$7.59/mo (`t3.micro` ~$7.59 → `t3.small` ~$15.18) — see the corrected Phase 12a total above.

**CI + operational monitoring, pulled forward with the deploy:** a deployed service nobody watches is not really "in production," and CI has no cloud dependency. **The split:** *operational* monitoring (up / fast / error-free — RED metrics) ships with the deploy; *ML* monitoring (drift, realised performance) needs the retrain infrastructure and real label/traffic volume, so it stays in 10b/13.
- **`.github/workflows/ci.yml`** — lint, `mypy --strict` on `src/`, `pytest --cov=src --cov-fail-under=80`, Docker build (no push) of both images. Runs on every push/PR from now. This is Phase 11's `ci.yml` in full.
- **`.github/workflows/cd.yml`, single-environment** — on merge to `main`: build + tag both images with the commit SHA, push to ECR, then **`aws ssm send-command`** to the instance running `cd /opt/telco-churn && docker compose pull && docker compose up -d`, then `scripts/smoke_test_serving.sh` against the public URL. SSM Run Command means **no SSH key in GitHub** — the runner assumes an IAM role scoped to `ssm:SendCommand` on that one instance. The staging/prod split + approval gate stay in Phase 11.
- **Grafana Cloud free tier** (10k series, 14-day retention) scraping the deployed `api` `/metrics` (exposed since Phase 9: `prometheus_fastapi_instrumentator` latency/rate/status + the five custom metrics — `predictions_total{scoring_path,model_version}`, `predicted_probability`, `predictions_above_threshold_total`, `champion_challenger_*`). One operational dashboard: request rate, latency p50/p99, 5xx rate, prediction volume by scoring path, score-distribution histogram. **Grafana Cloud rather than a Prometheus+Grafana pair on the box** — keeps ~200 MB off the 1 GB instance.

**Explicitly NOT pulled forward — stays in Phase 10b / 13:**
- **Evidently drift (`drift_check.py`)** — needs `prediction_log` volume + a `drift_reference.json` baseline `training_cycle.py` writes at promotion; on the static dataset PSI ≈ 0 forever (`ANALYSIS.md` §9 #13).
- **`performance_monitor.py` realised-performance / EV panels** — need matured labels (`prediction_outcomes`), produced only from 10a-ii on.
- **`data-quality.yml`** — Phase 11. **Least-privilege IAM audit beyond the instance role, DVC S3 remote** — Phase 12b. **App Runner/ECS migration and RDS multi-AZ are not built anywhere** — dropped 2026-09-16 as a documented `README.md` recommendation instead (see the deployment-target paragraph above).

**Doc ripples (same PR — capture the alternatives and the limits honestly):**
- **`docs/architecture.md`** — a "Deployment" section: the chosen shape (EC2 `t3.micro` + docker-compose + RDS + S3 + ECR, TLS via Caddy, CD via SSM) and a small comparison table against **App Runner** and **ECS-Fargate**. **Cost framing corrected 2026-09-16 — do not write the comparison as "EC2 = free vs. App Runner = $20+/mo."** This account has no classic free-tier quotas active (confirmed via its own Billing → Free Tier page), so the honest comparison is EC2+RDS+S3/ECR/EIP at **~$29/mo** vs. App Runner (2 always-on tasks, no EIP needed) + RDS + S3/ECR at roughly **~$34/mo** — App Runner costs *more*, not less. State the real reasons EC2 is chosen: more direct control, it's the harder and more instructive path for what this phase demonstrates, and — since App Runner is the pricier option here — there's no cost case for migrating later either. **This table is the reasoning `README.md` cites (Phase 12b) when it names App Runner/ECS-Fargate as a documented-not-built scale recommendation** — write the table so it stands on its own as that citation, not as a migration plan; this project does not build the migration.
- **`ANALYSIS.md` §9** — **two** new items under *Data & Production-Readiness Constraints*, not one: (1) the single-instance deployment is a real production deployment but not HA — single point of failure, single-AZ RDS, brief drop on `docker compose up -d`, burstable CPU throttles under sustained load, host OS patched manually. State the mitigations (auto-restart, systemd on boot, RDS automated backups) and the Phase 12b upgrade path. (2) **Added 2026-09-16 — a distinct risk, not a variant of (1):** this AWS account is on the newer Free Plan, metering all usage against a shared, finite credit balance rather than a classic free tier; per the account's own language, service **access ends outright** when credits deplete or the plan period expires, rather than gracefully starting to bill a card. That's an operational cliff, not a gradual degradation — unlike (1)'s resilience gaps, which fail partially and recoverably, this one fails the whole deployment at once, on a schedule outside this project's control (the credit pool is shared with other work on the account). State the mitigation (the calendar reminder + cost-minimization levers in `PROJECT_PLAN.md` Group 0) and that it has no code-level fix, only an operational one.
- **`README.md`** — one line in the project-status / architecture blurb: "v1 deployed on AWS (EC2 + docker-compose)." **Corrected 2026-09-16 — drop "free-tier" from this line entirely**, it was about to publish a factually wrong claim to a public, recruiter-facing document: this account's Billing → Free Tier page confirms no classic free-tier quotas are active, so the deployment costs ~$29/mo, metered against shared credits, not free. Plus the live URL once it exists. **The App Runner/ECS-Fargate "scale target" phrasing moves to Phase 12b's own README deliverable** (a short "Scaling this further" note, not a claim about what's deployed) — this line states only what's actually running.
- **`CLAUDE.md`** — the "Deployment | AWS App Runner" architecture-table row and any prose naming App Runner as *the* target become "EC2 + docker-compose (12a); App Runner / ECS-Fargate is a documented-not-built scale recommendation, see `README.md`." The `auth.enabled` flip rationale in § Serving is unchanged — it just happens on EC2 now.

**Build order — individual task breakdown.** Expands the deliverables above into an ordered, checkable sequence. The fifteen groups (0–13, plus 6.5) map to the AWS concept areas a deploy like this rests on; do them roughly top to bottom, since each group depends on the ones above it. `terraform apply` is called out where a group is a natural apply boundary — apply in stages so a failure is localised.

> **📌 This checklist is the original pre-build plan, frozen as written — it is not kept checked off day to day and several specifics below are now superseded (e.g. the `t3.micro`/Amazon Linux/SSH-port-22 bullet above).** Live, day-to-day task tracking for Phase 12a happens in **`docs/PHASE_12A_TODO.md`**, which is itself ~90% checked off as of 2026-10-19 (Groups 0–11 done and verified against the real deployed box; Group 12 monitoring and Group 13 doc ripples remain). Read this section for the original reasoning; read that file for current status.

*0. Account prerequisites (one-time — console + laptop):*
- [x] Enable MFA on the account root user; store recovery codes offline; stop using root after setup
- [x] **Not IAM Identity Center — ruled out both ways, confirmed 2026-09-16.** Enabling it via AWS Organizations auto-upgrades this account off its Free Plan and forfeits the credit balance immediately; the alternative, an Identity Center *account instance*, avoids the Organizations trigger but doesn't support permission sets, so it structurally cannot grant AWS account access at all. Neither shape works for a solo admin identity without either paying for it or being unable to log in with it.
- [x] Create IAM user `telco-churn-admin` (plain IAM); enable MFA on the user. The user carries **no direct permissions** — only `sts:AssumeRole` on the `terraform-admin` role below, so its access key alone grants nothing.
- [x] Create IAM role `terraform-admin` with `AdministratorAccess` attached; trust policy scoped to `telco-churn-admin`'s principal **with an `aws:MultiFactorAuthPresent` condition** — this role, not the user, is the actual admin identity
- [x] Install AWS CLI v2 and Terraform/OpenTofu locally; generate one access key for `telco-churn-admin`, then configure `~/.aws/config` as a two-profile chain (base profile holding the key → `telco-churn` profile with `role_arn`/`source_profile`/`mfa_serial`) so every session calls `aws sts assume-role` under the hood, prompting for an MFA code and returning short-lived STS creds — **no usable long-lived admin credential on the laptop**, since the base key alone can only call `AssumeRole`
- [x] Install the AWS Session Manager plugin for the CLI — needed for every keyless shell/port-forward path this plan relies on (Group 8's Session Manager access, Group 6.5's RDS tunnel), not just an optional convenience
- [x] Fix the region (`us-east-1`) and use it for every resource this project creates — independent of any other project's resources in a different region on the same account
- [x] Confirm the account's credit balance and expiry date in Billing Dashboard → Credits (not the classic 12-month free tier — **this account is on AWS's revamped Free Plan: a card is on file, but the account does not silently roll to metered billing on that card. AWS's own console language is that access to services ends when credits are depleted or the free period ends, unless the account is explicitly upgraded to a Paid Plan first** — confirmed via the account's own Billing dashboard, 2026-09-16. This is the opposite risk from a runaway bill: the failure mode to actually guard against here is an unannounced outage of the 24/7 service this phase exists to build, not overspend — the billing alarms below protect against the wrong thing if this account never gets upgraded to a Paid Plan)
- [x] Billing alarms scaled to the actual credit balance (**confirmed $155.32 remaining, 124 days left, expiring 2027-01-16** — shared across every project on this account, not this project's own budget) rather than a token amount: `EstimatedCharges > $100` and `> $140` (us-east-1) **and** an AWS Budgets budget at the same two thresholds with 50/80/100% alerts — before creating any billable resource (necessarily account-wide; AWS billing metrics aren't scoped per-IAM-user/project without tag-based cost allocation, which is out of scope here). **These alarms only matter if/when the account is on a Paid Plan** — on Free Plan they'll likely never fire, since spend stops accruing once access ends rather than exceeding a dollar threshold.
- [x] **A second, separate calendar reminder well before 2027-01-16 (set for 2026-12-31) to make an explicit decision: upgrade to AWS's Paid Plan to keep serving past the credit/free-period cutoff, or run Group 12's teardown ahead of that date.** Left undecided, the deadline arriving un-triaged means the service goes dark with no warning — the account's own language is "access... will end," not "billing continues," so there is no silent overspend to alert on, only a silent outage to pre-empt. This is distinct from, and more urgent than, Group 12's existing classic-12-month-free-tier reminder below, which runs on a different (per-resource-age) clock.
- [ ] **Standing habit, not a one-time task — applies only during the Groups 1–11 build/test window, retired once Final Verification confirms genuine 24/7 uptime:** stop (not terminate) the EC2 instance when not actively developing — EC2 compute cost drops to ~$0 while stopped, vs. ~$28/mo combined EC2+RDS (post Group 9's `t3.small` bump: EC2 ~$15.18 + RDS ~$12.41). **This is not a hypothetical "if free-tier coverage doesn't apply" — confirmed 2026-09-16 it doesn't, so this ~$28/mo (closer to ~$36–38/mo with EBS/EIP/S3/ECR included — see line 607's itemized figure) is the account's actual, ongoing metered cost from the moment these resources exist.**
- [x] **Cost-minimization levers, ranked biggest to smallest, since these credits are finite and shared with other projects on the account:**
  1. **❌ Declined (2026-09-16): stop (not terminate) EC2+RDS whenever the deployment isn't actively being shown to someone, extending the habit above past the building/testing window into the "24/7 demo" window too.** Would cut ~$28/mo of the ~$36–38 total (EC2 ~$15.18 post-`t3.small` + RDS ~$12.41) to near-zero compute cost while stopped (storage still bills, a few dollars/mo) — but the link goes on the CV and GitHub profile, where a recruiter can click it at any time with no demo window to schedule around. "Up on demand" means the link is broken most of the time it's actually clicked, which defeats the point of deploying it. **Decision: accept ~$36–38/mo for genuine always-on availability.**
  2. **❌ Considered and declined (2026-09-16): Graviton (ARM) instance types** — `t4g.micro` (EC2) and `db.t4g.micro` (RDS) instead of the `t3` equivalents, ~10–20% cheaper for equivalent burstable performance. Not actually a "free win" once the real cost is counted: it needs a genuine multi-arch Docker pipeline (`docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64` in both Group 10's CI and Group 11's CD, replacing the plain `docker build` steps), slower CI builds, and an ongoing risk surface (a future dependency lacking an arm64 wheel breaks the build with no warning). **Decision: stay on `t3.micro`/`db.t3.micro`** — ~$2–4/mo saved doesn't clear the bar against the setup work and added CI complexity, especially with real backlog still ahead in this same phase (Groups 11+, doc ripples, Phase 10b). Easy to revisit later if the cost picture changes; not a permanent rule-out.
  3. **❌ Considered and declined (2026-09-16): run Postgres in a fourth container on the EC2 box instead of RDS.** The naive "~$14.71/mo saved, roughly half the bill" estimate assumed keeping `t3.micro` — not actually safe: Postgres pushes the box's baseline RAM (already ~700–850 MB of 1 GB with just `api`/`ui`/`caddy`/the CloudWatch agent, per Group 9's own budget) to ~800 MB–1.05 GB, at or over the ceiling with no margin for load spikes. A safe version needs upsizing to `t3.small` (2 GB, ~$15.18/mo compute), which claws back most of the saving — the real number is **~$6/mo (~20%)**, not ~$14.71/mo, and it costs RDS's managed backups/patching on top. **Decision: stay on RDS at ~$29/mo** — the modest, correctly-recalculated saving doesn't justify giving up managed backups/patching or absorbing the added operational complexity of self-hosting Postgres in the deployment's most resource-constrained component.
  4. **The Elastic IP (~$3.65/mo) is not a place to cut.** Caddy's automatic TLS and DuckDNS both need a stable public IP that survives a stop/start cycle — only an EIP guarantees that (a plain auto-assigned public IP changes on stop/start, breaking DNS until manually re-pointed). Removing it would undermine the "reboot brings itself back with no manual step" verification goal, and lever 1 above already needs it if EC2 gets stopped/started routinely.
  5. S3/ECR/data-transfer (~$1–2/mo) are already minimized by the lifecycle policies in Groups 4/5 — nothing further to cut there.
- [x] Free domain via DuckDNS — needed because Caddy's automatic Let's Encrypt TLS requires a real hostname, not a bare IP: sign in at duckdns.org with an existing account (Google/GitHub/etc.), type a subdomain name (e.g. `telco-churn`) and click "add domain" to reserve `telco-churn.duckdns.org`, then save the token shown on the dashboard (used once, later, to point the name at the server's IP — see step 8). A paid domain works the same way and is the more polished-looking alternative, at a few dollars/year.

*1. Terraform scaffold + remote state:*
- [x] Bootstrap the state backend — an S3 bucket (versioning + SSE + Block Public Access on) plus a lock (S3 native lockfile or a DynamoDB table), created by hand or a throwaway `infra/bootstrap/` config with local state
- [x] `infra/` root module: `versions.tf` (required version, provider pin, `backend "s3"`), `providers.tf` (region + `default_tags`: Project / Environment / ManagedBy), `variables.tf`, `outputs.tf`
- [x] `terraform init`; commit `.terraform.lock.hcl`; gitignore `*.tfstate*`, `.terraform/`, secret `*.tfvars`
- [x] Per-concern file split: `network.tf`, `iam.tf`, `compute.tf`, `data.tf`, `ecr.tf`, `ssm.tf`, `monitoring.tf`

*2. IAM:*
- [x] EC2 instance role + instance profile; trust policy for `ec2.amazonaws.com`
- [x] Instance permission policy, least-privilege: `ecr:GetAuthorizationToken` on `*`; ECR pull actions on the two repo ARNs; `s3:GetObject`/`PutObject`/`ListBucket` on the artifact-bucket ARN; `ssm:GetParameter*` on `parameter/telco-churn/*`; `kms:Decrypt` on the `aws/ssm` key; CloudWatch-agent actions (`cloudwatch:PutMetricData`, `logs:CreateLogGroup`/`CreateLogStream`/`PutLogEvents`); `ssmmessages`/`ssm` for Session Manager + Run Command
- [x] GitHub OIDC provider (`token.actions.githubusercontent.com`, audience `sts.amazonaws.com`)
- [x] CI deploy role: trust policy scoped to `repo:<org>/<repo>:ref:refs/heads/main`; permission policy = ECR push on the two repos + `ssm:SendCommand` (scoped by the `Project` resource tag, not the instance ARN — see the next bullet) on the `AWS-RunShellScript` document ARN + `ssm:GetCommandInvocation`
- [x] **The ECR/S3/instance ARNs above are forward references — this group's own `terraform apply` runs before ecr.tf (Group 5), data.tf's bucket (Group 4), or compute.tf's instance (Group 8) exist, so an `aws_ecr_repository.*.arn`/`aws_s3_bucket.*.arn`/`aws_instance.*.arn` attribute reference would fail as an undeclared resource.** ECR and S3 ARNs are predictable from names this plan already fixes (`arn:aws:ecr:<region>:<account_id>:repository/telco-churn/{api,ui}`, `arn:aws:s3:::telco-churn-mlflow`), so write those two as interpolated string literals, not resource references. The EC2 instance ARN has no such fix — its ID is AWS-assigned at creation (Group 8), unknowable this early — so scope the CI role's `ssm:SendCommand` by a `Condition` on `ssm:resourceTag/Project = telco-churn` instead of a specific instance ARN; this is also the more durable choice, since it survives the instance being replaced later without an IAM edit.
- [x] `terraform apply`; grep the plan for stray `iam:*` / `Resource: "*"` wildcards

*3. Networking (VPC):*
- [x] `aws_vpc` `10.0.0.0/16`; enable DNS hostnames + support
- [x] Two public + two private subnets across two AZs
- [x] Internet gateway; public route table (`0.0.0.0/0 → igw`) + associations; private route table (local route only)
- [x] `aws_security_group` **ec2**: in `80` + `443` from `0.0.0.0/0`, out all — no inbound `22`; shell access goes through SSM Session Manager only (keyless, no open port, full CloudTrail audit trail — see Step 2's instance-role `ssmmessages`/`ssm` permissions)
- [x] `aws_security_group` **rds**: in `5432` from the ec2 SG id only
- [x] S3 gateway VPC endpoint attached to the route tables (free; keeps artifact traffic off the IGW)
- [x] `aws_eip` for the instance
- [x] `terraform apply`

*4. S3 (MLflow artifact store):*
- [x] `aws_s3_bucket` with a globally-unique suffix; `force_destroy = true`
- [x] Public-access-block (all four true); SSE-S3; versioning enabled
- [x] Lifecycle: expire noncurrent versions after 30d; abort incomplete multipart uploads after 7d
- [x] Bucket policy: deny non-TLS; restrict principals to the instance-role ARN
- [x] `terraform apply`; confirm the instance role can list it and anonymous access `403`s
- [x] Note: the bucket's internal layout (`<experiment_id>/<run_id>/artifacts/...`) is MLflow-managed, opaque IDs only — never a hand-designed prefix scheme. Always inspect contents via the MLflow UI (which joins these IDs against Postgres's run/experiment names), never the S3 console directly

*5. ECR:*
- [x] `aws_ecr_repository` `telco-churn/api` and `telco-churn/ui`: `scan_on_push = true`, `image_tag_mutability = IMMUTABLE`, `force_delete = true`
- [x] `aws_ecr_lifecycle_policy` on each: expire untagged after 7d, keep last 5 tagged
- [x] `terraform apply`; one manual `docker login` + build + push of each image to prove the path

*6. RDS:*
- [x] `aws_db_subnet_group` over the two private subnets
- [x] Optional `aws_db_parameter_group` with `rds.force_ssl = 1`
- [x] `aws_db_instance`: `engine = postgres`, `db.t3.micro`, `allocated_storage = 20`, **`gp3` — reversed 2026-09-16, confirmed against current RDS pricing.** The original `gp2` call rested entirely on "the free tier's 20 GB allowance is `gp2`-only," a premise this document has since retracted (Group 0's account-status check found no free-tier-eligible instances on this account at all). With that premise gone, the two are a plain price/performance comparison, not a free-vs-paid one — and `gp3` wins outright: both bill storage at the identical **$0.115/GB-month** in `us-east-1`, but `gp3` includes a flat **3,000 IOPS / 125 MB/s baseline free for any volume under 400 GiB** (this one is 20 GB), while `gp2`'s IOPS scale with size at 3 IOPS/GB — a 20 GB `gp2` volume gets only ~60 IOPS (below its own 100 IOPS floor), roughly 1/30th of what `gp3` gives for the same price. No functional trade-off either way at this single-operator `db.t3.micro`'s actual load, but there is no longer any reason to pick `gp2`. `multi_az = false`, `storage_encrypted = true`, `publicly_accessible = false`, `backup_retention_period = 7`, `deletion_protection = true`, `final_snapshot_identifier` set, master password from a `random_password` resource (`length = 32`, `special = false` — avoids characters that break a connection-string URL), **not** a human-supplied `TF_VAR_`. Industry-standard practice here is actually RDS's own `manage_master_user_password = true` (AWS-generated, rotated, stored in Secrets Manager) — deliberately not used, since it forces Secrets Manager back in and Group 0/12a already ruled that out on cost (`SSM Parameter Store (not Secrets Manager — SSM standard params are free)`). `random_password` is the standard fallback once Secrets Manager is off the table: no human ever types, copies, or sees the master password, the same no-plaintext-handling property Secrets Manager would have given, just without its per-secret fee.
- [x] `terraform apply`
- [x] From the box (once it exists) or a temporary in-VPC shell: `psql` → `CREATE DATABASE mlflow;`
- [x] `alembic upgrade head` against the app DB, once
- [x] No manual SQLAlchemy-URL assembly step — Group 7's `/telco-churn/rds-url` SSM parameter is itself a Terraform resource, its `value` interpolated directly from `random_password.rds_master.result` + `aws_db_instance.main.endpoint`. The URL never leaves Terraform state to be hand-carried into the next group.

*6.5. Data bootstrap (rebuild fresh, not migrate):*
> **✅ RESOLVED — network path and execution order.** `publicly_accessible = false` means this group cannot actually run where it's listed — the natural jump point into the private subnet is the EC2 box, which doesn't exist until Group 8. **This group's steps execute after Group 8, once the box and its SSM Agent exist** — it stays numbered/located here only because it's conceptually "finish what RDS started," the same non-literal ordering Group 6's own `psql` bullet already uses. Mechanism: `aws ssm start-session --target <instance-id> --document-name AWS-StartPortForwardingSessionToRemoteHost --parameters host="<rds-endpoint>",portNumber="5432",localPortNumber="5432"` tunnels RDS's private endpoint to `localhost:5432` through the same SSM Agent already used for shell access and CD — no bastion host, no briefly-flipping `publicly_accessible = true`. Everything below then runs as the *exact same local `uv run` commands used throughout the rest of this project*, just pointed at the tunnel instead of local Docker Postgres. Requires the AWS Session Manager plugin installed locally (Group 0) — the one new laptop prerequisite this adds.
- [x] Point `data/ingest.py` at the new RDS app database (via the tunnel) and re-run it — reproduces `customers_raw` from the static, committed CSV; deterministic, nothing copied over from the laptop
- [x] `make crm-data` against the same RDS connection — regenerates `customers_crm` (seeded, so the nudges match every prior run)
- [x] **Real gap found 2026-09-16, before running any training against this backend — an MLflow experiment's `artifact_location` is decided once, permanently, at first creation, and is never touched again.** Locally that default is owned by the running `mlflow server` process's `--default-artifact-root`; this backend runs with no server in front of it at all (direct-Postgres-URI mode, per this document's own "no MLflow server container" decision), so the very first `mlflow.set_experiment("telco-churn-training")` call against this brand-new, empty `mlflow` database would otherwise silently default to a local `./mlruns` folder on whatever machine happened to touch it first — wrong for a backend the EC2 box and every future retrain must also be able to read from. **Fixed in code, not a one-off manual step (the whole point of this bootstrap is not trusting anything only a human remembers to do):** `configs/config.yaml` gained `mlflow.artifact_location: ${oc.env:MLFLOW_ARTIFACT_LOCATION,''}`, and `utils/mlflow.py::ensure_experiment_metadata` — the single choke point every training-cycle module already calls before touching MLflow — now explicitly `client.create_experiment(name, artifact_location=...)`s the moment it finds the experiment doesn't yet exist, only when this config value is non-empty; an already-existing experiment is never touched (its location is immutable anyway). Local/CI are unaffected — the env var is unset there, so behavior is identical to before.
- [x] **Second gap found the same way, same day: `boto3` was missing from the dependency tree entirely** (confirmed absent even transitively from `uv.lock`). MLflow's S3 artifact repository needs it installed to do anything against an `s3://` artifact_location at all — without it, the training cycle below fails the instant it tries to log the model, and `serving/predict.py::resolve_champion_model` / `ui/streamlit_app.py`'s `mlflow.artifacts.load_dict` (for `model_card.json`) would fail identically in production. Added `boto3>=1.35` to `pyproject.toml`'s **base** `dependencies` — not extras-gated, since training, `serving`, and `ui` all need it (training writes artifacts; `api` loads the champion model; `ui` reads `model_card.json`) — and re-ran `uv sync --all-extras`. Any image built before this change (Group 5's disposable `manual-test` proof images, already deleted) predates it and would be missing `boto3` — a real rebuild is needed once Group 9/11 build the real, SHA-tagged images.
- [x] **Added 2026-09-16 — re-run `candidates.py`/`comparison.py` (`notebooks/03a-model-selection.ipynb`) and the feature-selection ablation (`notebooks/03b-feature-selection.ipynb`) once against this same RDS+S3 backend, before the five-step cycle below, purely so a reviewer opening the *deployed* system's MLflow instance can see the comparison evidence behind `COMMITTED_MODEL_FAMILY`/`COMMITTED_FEATURES`, not just a pipeline that starts from "already decided" with no visible justification in that instance.** Sequenced first for a second reason too: these two notebooks only ever *read* from the new backend (no registry writes, no `champion`/`challenger` alias touched) — running them first is a lower-stakes way to confirm the `artifact_location`/`boto3` fix above actually round-trips a real S3 write/read before risking the five-step cycle's registry-mutating writes on a backend that's only been unit-tested, never proven against real AWS. This is cosmetic/evidentiary, not decisional: these two notebooks are explicitly notebook-only, on-demand tools never wired into the automated pipeline, and their own decision-check logic (`03a`'s `today_family == COMMITTED_MODEL_FAMILY` branch) compares today's fresh computation against the hardcoded Python constant, never against the *original* decision run — so it works identically regardless of which MLflow backend/experiment logs it, and regardless of whether a champion has been minted there yet. **Do not update `COMMITTED_MODEL_FAMILY_DECISION_RUN_ID`/`COMMITTED_FEATURES_DECISION_RUN_ID` after this re-run** unless the notebook actually prints "DECISION CHANGED" (which would mean something is genuinely wrong, not something to silently accept) — the expected, normal outcome is "Reconfirmed — no change needed," in which case `models/train/common.py`/`features/schema.py` stay exactly as they are, and the original citation (the run that was actually reviewed, per `ANALYSIS.md §4a`) remains authoritative. Today's run is additional confirming evidence living alongside it, in a different experiment, never a replacement for it.
- [x] Point `MLFLOW_TRACKING_URI` / `MLFLOW_ARTIFACT_LOCATION` at the new RDS `mlflow` database + S3 bucket, then run the same **five-step manual cycle** the README's Quick Start and `CLAUDE.md` already document — stage-targeted, not a bare `dvc repro` — with `--force` on the *first* call only:
  ```
  dvc repro --force calibrate   # force required: see below
  register.py                   # mint challenger, tag pending
  dvc repro error_analysis      # NOT forced — see below
  models.review
  register.py register.model_version=<n>   # promote
  ```
  to mint a fresh `champion` in the new backend rather than migrating the old one; same seeds + same static CSV reproduce the same model. **`--force` on the first call only, never the second — the two have opposite needs:** run from the same laptop that already built the model against local Postgres, the local `reports/*_receipt.json` files from those earlier runs still exist and hash-match, so a plain (unforced) `dvc repro calibrate` would see `ingest`'s output as already present against the *old* backend and silently skip it, even though the new RDS `customers_raw` table is empty; this is the same failure mode CLAUDE.md already documents for a hand-edited `customers_raw` (§ Data Handling: *"invisible to `dvc repro` — rebuild with `dvc repro --force`"*). The second call must **not** be forced, though: `dvc repro --force error_analysis` would force its entire upstream chain too, re-running `calibrate` a *third* time and producing a new run_id different from the one `register.py` already minted a challenger version from in between — silently decoupling the registry entry from what actually gets evaluated. It doesn't need forcing anyway: MLflow run_ids are randomly generated per run, so the first call's fresh `calibrate` run genuinely changed `reports/calibrate_receipt.json`'s content — and since that receipt is a declared dependency of `threshold`/`evaluate`/`error_analysis`, DVC's ordinary (unforced) staleness check already sees it changed and reruns that chain correctly on its own.
- [x] Confirm `resolve_champion_model` finds and loads the new champion before pointing any traffic at the box

*7. SSM Parameter Store:*
- [x] **Four ownership models across nine parameters, not two — each mismatch here is a real Terraform-fights-reality bug, not a style choice:**
  - **Human-generated, `ignore_changes` required:** `/telco-churn/api-key` (SecureString, `openssl rand -hex 32`) — an app-level secret Terraform must never silently regenerate out from under a running deployment. **Same category, added 2026-09-16:** `/telco-churn/grafana-endpoint` and `/telco-churn/grafana-api-key` (SecureString) — Group 12's Grafana Alloy needs both to `remote_write` to Grafana Cloud; both come from the Grafana Cloud console (not derivable from any Terraform-managed resource), so they're set once by hand the same way `api-key` is. No new IAM grant needed — Group 2's `parameter/telco-churn/*` wildcard already covers them. **Corrected 2026-10-19:** a real Grafana Cloud stack confirmed `remote_write` needs HTTP basic_auth (username = numeric Stack ID), not a bare bearer token as originally assumed — added a third same-category param, `/telco-churn/grafana-username` (String, not secret), bringing the real count to ten.
  - **CD-mutated, `ignore_changes` required — same protection as `api-key`, different reason:** `/telco-churn/api-image`, `/telco-churn/ui-image` (String) — Group 11's CD workflow updates these on every merge to `main`. Without `lifecycle { ignore_changes = [value] }`, the next `terraform apply` for any unrelated reason (a Group 12b infra tweak, a stray `apply` after a drift-checking `plan`) would see CD's update as configuration drift and silently revert it to whatever's hardcoded in the `.tf` file — redeploying a stale or wrong image the next time the box refreshes its env.
  - **Fully Terraform-derived, no `ignore_changes` — self-corrects on purpose:** `/telco-churn/rds-url-mlflow` and `/telco-churn/rds-url-app` (both SecureString, from Group 6's `random_password`/`aws_db_instance` outputs, differing only in the trailing db-name path segment — `.../mlflow` vs `.../<app db>`) and `/telco-churn/s3-bucket` (String, from `aws_s3_bucket.mlflow.id` — Group 4 gives the bucket a globally-unique suffix, so Terraform picks the actual final name; no human can correctly hand-type this into `aws ssm put-parameter`). **Split from a single `/telco-churn/rds-url`, corrected 2026-09-16:** a Postgres connection string bakes in one specific database name, and this RDS instance holds two databases on the same host (`mlflow` and the app DB) — one string cannot resolve to both, so `api` needs two distinct URIs sharing the same host/credentials, not one shared string doing double duty.
  - **Genuinely static, plain resource:** `/telco-churn/domain` (String) — a human-picked literal from Group 0's DuckDNS step (one subdomain, serving both `api` and `ui` behind path-based routing — see Group 9's Caddyfile note), the one parameter here actually fitting "set via `aws ssm put-parameter` or a plain Terraform resource, either works."
- [x] `refresh-env.sh` on the box: `get-parameters-by-path --with-decryption --recursive` → render `/opt/telco-churn/.env` (chmod 600) — the two `rds-url-*` params render as `MLFLOW_TRACKING_URI` and `POSTGRES_URL` respectively, not both under one key
- [x] Confirm `--with-decryption` succeeds under the instance role (i.e. `kms:Decrypt` is actually attached)

*8. EC2 + first boot:*
> **OS choice: Ubuntu, not Amazon Linux 2023 (switched 2026-09-16).** AL2023 has real advantages for an AWS-only deployment — SSM Agent and the CloudWatch agent are native to it, patched on AWS's own cadence, and it has a leaner idle footprint (a real plus on a RAM-tight `t3.micro`). Ubuntu wins on two things that matter more here: it's the distro most reviewers/tutorials assume (`apt`, not AL2023's `dnf`/RPM), and it isn't AWS-specific if this deployment ever needs to move. Canonical's official Ubuntu Server AMIs ship the SSM Agent pre-installed too, so the SSM-everywhere design (Session Manager, Run Command, the Group 6.5 RDS tunnel) is unaffected either way.
- [x] `data.aws_ssm_parameter` for the latest Ubuntu 24.04 LTS AMI (Canonical's published SSM path, e.g. `/aws/service/canonical/ubuntu/server/24.04/stable/current/amd64/hvm/ebs-gp3/ami-id`)
- [x] `aws_instance`: `t3.micro`, public subnet, ec2 SG, instance profile, no `key_name` (no SSH keypair needed — SSM Session Manager is the only shell-access path), `metadata_options { http_tokens = "required" }`, 20 GB gp3 root
- [x] `aws_eip_association`
- [x] Point the DuckDNS record at the Elastic IP now that it exists — via the dashboard, or `curl "https://www.duckdns.org/update?domains=telco-churn&token=<token>&ip=<elastic-ip>"` — a one-time set, not a recurring updater, since the Elastic IP is static and won't change on reboot (unlike DuckDNS's typical home-router use case); confirm with `nslookup telco-churn.duckdns.org`
> **Config-file provisioning: Terraform owns `compose.prod.yml`/`refresh-env.sh`/`Caddyfile` as `aws_s3_object` resources, uploaded to a `deploy-config/` prefix in Group 4's S3 bucket — not baked into user-data, not git-cloned onto the box.** Nothing previously described how these three files reach `/opt/telco-churn/` at all: user-data created the directory and the systemd unit that references them, but never placed them there, and Group 11's CD command assumes they already exist. `aws_s3_object`'s content hash means a `terraform apply` re-uploads them whenever the local file changes, and the instance role already has `s3:GetObject`/`ListBucket` on this bucket (Group 2) — no new IAM grant needed. User-data downloads them once at first boot; Group 11's CD command re-syncs them on every deploy, so an edit to `compose.prod.yml` ships the same way an image-tag update does, not just at first boot.
- [x] user-data: `apt-get update`; install Docker + compose plugin from Docker's official `apt` repo (not Ubuntu-archive's older `docker.io` package — needed for the compose plugin); `systemctl enable --now docker`; install the CloudWatch agent (`.deb` package, Canonical/Amazon both publish one); create `/opt/telco-churn`; `aws s3 cp s3://<bucket>/deploy-config/ /opt/telco-churn/ --recursive` to fetch `compose.prod.yml`/`refresh-env.sh`/`Caddyfile`; install the `telco-churn.service` systemd unit (`ExecStartPre` = `refresh-env.sh`, `ExecStart` = `docker compose -f compose.prod.yml up -d`) and `systemctl enable` it
- [x] `terraform apply`; connect via Session Manager (or SSH); verify Docker + the agent are running

*9. Serving stack (`compose.prod.yml` on the box):*
- [x] `compose.prod.yml`: `api`, `ui`, `caddy`, `mlflow`; `restart: unless-stopped` on all; `env_file: [.env]`; images via `${API_IMAGE}` / `${UI_IMAGE}`. **This file only pulls and runs pre-built images for `api`/`ui` — no `build:` block for either.** The images themselves are built and pushed to ECR by `cd.yml` (Group 11) via plain `docker build`/`docker push` against `docker/api/Dockerfile`/`docker/ui/Dockerfile`, never via this compose file; `compose.prod.yml` only ever sees the resulting tags through `${API_IMAGE}`/`${UI_IMAGE}`. **`mlflow` is the one exception with a real `build:` block** — same `docker/mlflow/Dockerfile` local dev already uses, since it's a viewer-only service with no CD pipeline of its own.
- [x] **Logging: `awslogs` driver per service, not the default `json-file` driver — corrected 2026-09-16.** `logging: { driver: awslogs, options: { awslogs-group: /telco-churn/<service>, awslogs-region: us-east-1, awslogs-stream: <service> } }` ships `structlog`'s JSON lines straight to CloudWatch Logs with no Docker envelope wrapping them. The earlier `json-file` + `max-size`/`max-file` framing meant the CloudWatch agent would have had to tail local rotating log files and unwrap a nested `{"log": "<line>", ...}` envelope just to reach `structlog`'s own fields — `awslogs` skips that entirely, and needs no new IAM grant (Group 2's instance role already has `logs:CreateLogGroup`/`CreateLogStream`/`PutLogEvents`, originally granted for the CloudWatch agent — the same actions the driver itself calls).
- [x] `api`: `MLFLOW_TRACKING_URI=postgresql://…/mlflow` (direct DB URI, no server) **and** `POSTGRES_URL=postgresql://…/<app db>` (both from Group 7's two `rds-url-*` params — same host/credentials, different db name), `auth.enabled=true` Hydra override, `API_KEY` from env, healthcheck on `/ready`
- [x] **Image-size / dependency-footprint review — found 2026-09-16 during Group 5's ECR proof-push, real measured sizes: `api` ~1.01GB, `ui` ~860MB.** Ranked the built `api` image's `site-packages` by size (`du -sh`) for real evidence rather than guessing. `llvmlite`+`numba` (~206MB) is SHAP's JIT-compiler chain and `pyarrow` (153MB) is a real parquet dependency — both legitimate, not waste. Three real findings:
  - **`mlflow-skinny` swap, for the `api`/`ui` images specifically.** Full `mlflow` transitively pulls `matplotlib`+`fontTools`+`pillow`+`kiwisolver` (~63MB, confirmed via the size ranking — absent from the `serving` extra, only reachable through full `mlflow`) plus `flask`/`flask-cors`/`gunicorn`/`waitress`/`docker`/`graphene`/`aiohttp`/`cryptography`/`huey`/`skops` (per `uv.lock`) — the tracking-server/UI dependency surface, unused in `api`/`ui` since **neither of those two images ever runs an MLflow server itself** (both use direct-Postgres-URI client mode against RDS). This is unaffected by the separate `mlflow` service added to `compose.prod.yml` below — that's a different image entirely (`docker/mlflow/Dockerfile`, its own `build:` block), and genuinely needs full `mlflow`'s Flask/gunicorn to serve the UI. `mlflow-skinny` already covers `numpy`/`pandas`/`scikit-learn`/`scipy`/`sqlparse` (notably *not* `pyarrow` — see next finding); `sqlalchemy`/`alembic` are separate base deps for other reasons. **Gate before swapping:** confirm `mlflow-skinny` genuinely supports a direct-Postgres-URI tracking store (`SqlAlchemyStore`) against this exact `MLFLOW_TRACKING_URI` shape — untestable before this group's `compose.prod.yml` + RDS pairing exists.
  - **`pyarrow` (153MB) may be dead weight in `api`/`ui` specifically.** Confirmed via grep that neither `serving/` nor `ui/` import pyarrow or touch parquet directly; it's an unconditional base `dependencies` entry (not extras-gated) and also one of full `mlflow`'s own transitive deps (absent from `mlflow-skinny`'s list) — both images inherit it purely because the training/pipeline side of this same package needs it for `features/accessor.py::load_features()` and friends. Candidate for its own extra so serving images stop inheriting it, but that's a real dependency-architecture change, not a one-liner — feeds directly into this group's own "check 1GB RAM budget, drop `ui` to on-demand if tight" line below.
  - **Structural note, lower priority:** the UI's `sklearn`/`scipy` footprint (~157MB) traces to `models/artifacts.py`'s `resolve_champion_version` (all the UI needs) sharing a module with heavier loaders (`load_fitted_model`) that genuinely need `mlflow.sklearn`/`sklearn.pipeline.Pipeline` — importing the light function pays for the heavy ones' imports too. Fixable by splitting the module by weight, if it ever matters enough to bother.
- [x] **`docker/api/Dockerfile` gains `alembic.ini` and `alembic/` (added 2026-09-16, found reviewing Group 11's CD path):** currently the image copies only `src/`, `configs/`, `pyproject.toml` — `alembic` itself is installed (a base dependency) but has no config or migration scripts to run against inside the container. Without these two additions, there is no automated way to apply a schema migration at deploy time at all — see Group 11's migration step, which needs them present in the image it runs.
- [x] `ui`: the `_api_request` `X-API-Key` header fix (`headers={"X-API-Key": os.environ["API_KEY"], **kwargs.pop("headers", {})}` when the env var is set); check RAM with `api` loaded, drop `ui` to on-demand if tight
- [x] `Caddyfile`: **one hostname, one site block, path-based routing** — `/telco-churn/domain`'s single DuckDNS subdomain serves all three containers, no second DNS record or cert needed. `reverse_proxy api:8000` for the five known API routes (`/predict*`, `/health`, `/ready`, `/metrics`, `/customer/*`), `reverse_proxy mlflow:5000` for `/mlflow*` (gated by `basic_auth` — see below), `reverse_proxy ui:8501` for everything else. `ui` needs `--server.baseUrlPath` left at its default (mounted at `/`, not a sub-path) since it's the catch-all, not `api`'s or `mlflow`'s carve-out — no Streamlit config change needed. Named volumes for `/data` and `/config`; iterate against Let's Encrypt **staging**, then switch to production. **Corrected 2026-09-16 from "one site block per hostname"** — that phrasing assumed two hostnames nothing else in the plan provisions; this project has no reason (independent scaling, independent access control, a CDN in front of one but not the other) to want two.
- [x] **`mlflow` service, reviewer-facing MLflow UI — scoped 2026-09-18, reverses the "no MLflow server container" line this bullet used to carry.** `docker/mlflow/Dockerfile` + `boto3` (missing from the official MLflow image, same gap Group 6.5 found for the training tree), `--backend-store-uri "$MLFLOW_TRACKING_URI"` (already rendered by `refresh-env.sh` from the `rds-url-mlflow` SSM param), `--artifacts-destination s3://<bucket>/telco-churn-training --serve-artifacts`, `--static-prefix /mlflow` (shares the box's one hostname/cert instead of a second DNS record). Caddy's `basic_auth` on the `/mlflow*` route is the access control — MLflow's UI has no API-key concept like `api`'s `X-API-Key`. Publish a demo login in `README.md`, same justification as the API demo key (Group 13): the dataset is public/synthetic, so the point of auth is to demonstrate the control, not protect anything sensitive. This is the actual mechanism that lets a reviewer browse `03a`/`03b`'s live experiment evidence themselves, with no AWS credentials or SSM tunnel of their own.
- [x] Bring the stack up once by hand; confirm `@champion` resolves at boot against RDS + S3, `/ready` goes `503 → 200`, and `https://<domain>/mlflow` prompts for the basic-auth login and then renders the `telco-churn-training` experiment

*10. CI (`.github/workflows/ci.yml`) — already exists; this group closes three real gaps found reviewing it against `.pre-commit-config.yaml`/`pyproject.toml` (2026-09-16), not a from-scratch build:*
- [x] Already built: two jobs — `unit` (`actions/checkout` → `setup-python` 3.13 → `astral-sh/setup-uv` → `uv sync --frozen --all-extras` → `ruff check src/` → `mypy src/` → `pytest -m "not integration" -n auto`), gated `if: github.event_name != 'schedule'`; `integration` (same setup, `pytest -m integration --run-integration --no-cov -n auto`), gated to `schedule` (daily 06:13 UTC cron), `workflow_dispatch`, or a PR targeting `main` — a more mature design than earlier drafts of this plan described (integration tests were assumed deferred entirely to a separate Phase 11 `integration.yml`; in practice they're already a second job in this same file, on a lighter trigger).
- [x] **Gap 1 — no `pre-commit` step at all, confirmed against `.pre-commit-config.yaml`:** `ruff check`/`mypy` duplicate two of the file's hook categories, but `black`, `detect-secrets`, `nbstripout`/`upgrade-notebooks`/`fix-notebook-outputs`, and the basic hygiene hooks (`end-of-file-fixer`/`check-yaml`/`check-toml`/`trailing-whitespace`) only ever run if a contributor's local clone has hooks installed and isn't bypassed (`--no-verify`, a tool that skips git hooks entirely). Add `uv run pre-commit run --all-files` as its own step in the `unit` job — stays in sync with the config automatically rather than hand-duplicating each hook as a separate CI step.
- [x] **Gap 2 — nothing is SHA-pinned, confirmed against the real file:** `actions/checkout@v7`, `actions/setup-python@v7`, `astral-sh/setup-uv@v9.0.0` are all pinned to mutable version tags, not commit SHAs. A tag can be repointed after the fact (a compromised publisher account, a force-pushed tag); a SHA can't. Pin all three.
- [x] **Gap 3 — no `docker build` step for either image, though the plan has called for one since this group was first drafted:** add a `docker build` (no push) for both `docker/api/Dockerfile` and `docker/ui/Dockerfile` — catches a broken Dockerfile before it reaches `cd.yml` (Group 11), where a build failure means a failed production deploy instead of a failed PR check.
- [x] **One-time task, separate from Gap 3 — actually attempt `docker build` against both Dockerfiles now, before wiring the CI step in.** Gap 3 only prevents *future* breakage; it says nothing about whether either Dockerfile already fails to build *today*, since nothing has run `docker build` against either one since Phase 9 shipped — `uv.lock` has moved across Phases 10a-i/10a-ii with no verification catching a break. A real `docker build -f docker/api/Dockerfile .` / `docker build -f docker/ui/Dockerfile .` locally answers this empirically instead of assuming either still works.
- [x] Verify: a deliberate lint error makes it red; a deliberate `black`-only violation (correctly-linted but unformatted code) also makes it red, proving gap 1 is actually closed and not just present in the diff

*11. CD (`.github/workflows/cd.yml`, single-environment):*
- [x] `on: push: branches: [main]`; `permissions: id-token: write`; `concurrency: { group: deploy, cancel-in-progress: false }`
- [x] Steps: checkout → `configure-aws-credentials` (OIDC role) → ECR login → build + push `api`/`ui` tagged `${{ github.sha }}` (plain `docker build`/`docker push` against `docker/api/Dockerfile`/`docker/ui/Dockerfile` — `compose.prod.yml` plays no part in this half) → update `/telco-churn/{api,ui}-image` SSM params → `aws ssm send-command` (`cd /opt/telco-churn && aws s3 sync s3://<bucket>/deploy-config/ . && ./refresh-env.sh && docker compose pull && docker compose run --rm api alembic upgrade head && docker compose up -d && docker image prune -f`, this second half being the only place `compose.prod.yml` is actually invoked — the `s3 sync` picks up any Terraform-applied edit to `compose.prod.yml`/`refresh-env.sh`/`Caddyfile` since the last deploy (Group 8's config-provisioning note), `refresh-env.sh` re-renders `.env` from the SSM params just updated, `pull` fetches the new image tags, `alembic upgrade head` applies any pending migration **using the newly-pulled `api` image's own migration files — before** the new containers start serving, so nothing ever serves against a not-yet-migrated schema, `up -d` brings the new containers up, and **`docker image prune -f` (added 2026-09-16) removes the now-unreferenced old image layers** — without it, every deploy leaves the previous images' layers on the 20 GB root volume with nothing ever reclaiming them, a slow but real path to disk exhaustion on a box that redeploys on every merge to `main`) → poll `get-command-invocation` until terminal, fail on non-zero exit → `scripts/smoke_test_serving.sh https://<domain>`
- [x] **Closes a real gap found 2026-09-16 reviewing this group: without this, there was no automated path for schema migrations at all.** Group 6's `alembic upgrade head` was a one-time bootstrap step; nothing since then re-ran it. A future PR adding a migration (a real, expected occurrence — `prediction_outcomes`/`training_pool` etc. already exist from Phase 10a) would have deployed new code with no automated way to apply the schema change it needs, undermining Phase 12a's own "no laptop in the loop" goal the moment a migration was actually needed.
- [x] **`aws ssm send-command` only dispatches — it never executes `compose.prod.yml` itself.** The GitHub Actions runner has no network path to the box (no SSH key was ever provisioned there, by design) and never runs `docker compose` in-job; it hands the command string to the AWS SSM API, which relays it to the SSM Agent daemon already running on the EC2 instance (installed via Group 8's user-data). That agent is what actually executes `docker compose pull`/`up -d` locally, against the `compose.prod.yml` sitting on the box's own disk at `/opt/telco-churn/` — the one and only place that file is ever invoked. Control then returns to the runner, which polls `get-command-invocation` (an AWS API call) for the remote execution's terminal status, then runs the smoke test directly from the runner as plain HTTPS requests against the public domain — no box access needed for that part. So the split is: GitHub Actions = orchestrator (build/push, update SSM params, dispatch, poll, smoke-test from outside); the box's own SSM Agent = executor (the only thing that ever touches `compose.prod.yml`).
- [x] `scripts/smoke_test_serving.sh`: `/health` 200, `/ready` 200, `/predict` no key → 401, `/predict` with key → valid probability, UI root loads
- [x] **Superseded — this bullet originally deferred rollback to Phase 11, but it was actually built in 12a itself, ahead of this plan.** Per `docs/PHASE_12A_TODO.md` Group 11: automated rollback was built 2026-09-20 and drilled with a real injected failure 2026-09-21 — `cd.yml` records the previous `*-image` values before flipping, and on any failure from the flip onward it restores them, re-runs the deploy, re-smoke-tests, and stays red. Only the manual-approval gate (the other half of what this bullet called "Phase 11") remains genuinely deferred.
- [x] Verify: a merge to `main` deploys and smoke-tests green with no laptop involved

*12. Operational monitoring + cost:*
- [ ] `aws_cloudwatch_log_group` per service (retention 14d) — the `awslogs` driver (Group 9) writes to these directly, so the CloudWatch agent's job narrows to **host-level metrics only** (`mem_used_percent` / `disk_used_percent`), not container log tailing — corrected 2026-09-16, see Group 9's logging-driver note for why. CloudWatch-agent config via SSM param; `LOG_FORMAT=json` in the container env so `structlog` renders JSON that Logs Insights can query directly, with no nested envelope to unwrap
- [ ] `aws_sns_topic` + email subscription; metric alarms: `CPUUtilization > 90%`/15m, `StatusCheckFailed >= 1`, `mem_used_percent > 90`, **`disk_used_percent > 90`** (added 2026-09-16 — the agent already collects this metric per the bullet above, but nothing previously alarmed on it; Group 11's new `docker image prune -f` step reduces the risk but doesn't eliminate the need to actually watch it)
- [ ] Grafana Alloy on the box: scrape `api:8000/metrics` locally, `remote_write` to Grafana Cloud (endpoint + key from SSM)
- [ ] One Grafana dashboard: request rate, latency p50/p99, 5xx rate, prediction volume by route, `predicted_probability` histogram; alerts on p99, 5xx ratio, sustained `/ready` down
- [ ] Confirm `customerid` is not a label on any metric
- [ ] Calendar reminder for the classic 12-month free-tier expiry (separate, per-resource-age clock — **the nearer, harder deadline is Group 0's 2027-01-16 Free Plan credit/period cutoff**, which ends access outright rather than starting to bill). **Teardown is three steps, not one, because Group 6's RDS instance ships with `deletion_protection = true` and a `final_snapshot_identifier`:** (1) `aws rds modify-db-instance --db-instance-identifier <id> --no-deletion-protection --apply-immediately`, wait for it to apply; (2) `terraform destroy`, which will succeed and take the final snapshot on its way out; (3) **`aws rds delete-db-snapshot` on that snapshot in the same sitting, by default** — it is not a Terraform-tracked resource, so `destroy` never removes it, and it is a real ongoing charge (~$1.90/mo for 20 GB in `us-east-1`) against the **$155.32 in credits shared across every project on this account** (confirmed 2026-09-16), not a per-project budget. Its restore-point value is narrow — most of this instance's content (`customers_raw`/`customers_crm`/the MLflow registry) is deterministically regenerable from the committed CSV + fixed seeds per Group 6.5, so what it's actually protecting is the non-regenerable `prediction_log`/`prediction_outcomes` traffic history accrued since the box went live. Skip step 3 only with a deliberate, dated reason (e.g. "keep until 2026-10-01, reassess") — never as a silent default.

**Verification:** `curl https://<domain>/predict` with no `X-API-Key` → `401`; with the key → a churn probability. `/health` / `/ready` / `/metrics` need no key. The Streamlit URL loads and submits a prediction. `@champion` resolves at boot against RDS + S3 with no MLflow server running anywhere; `/ready` goes `503 → 200`. **Reboot the EC2 instance** — the systemd unit + `restart: unless-stopped` bring the stack back with no manual step. Close the laptop — the service keeps serving. A push with a deliberate lint error fails `ci.yml`; a merge to `main` deploys via `cd.yml`'s SSM path and the smoke test passes. The Grafana Cloud dashboard populates within minutes of driving traffic at the public URL. **The month's AWS cost is ~$36–38, metered against the account's shared Free Plan credit balance** — not $0 (this account has no classic free-tier quotas active, confirmed 2026-09-16; see Group 0 for the cost breakdown and minimization levers).

---

### Phase 10 — Orchestration (Continuous Training), split into four work packages

**Split rationale, added 2026-08-20 to sequence the work:** Phase 10's own deliverables interleave two genuinely different kinds of work — schema/code/policy that has nothing to do with Prefect and would need building the same way under cron or Airflow, versus the actual Prefect server, flow scheduling, and suspend/resume mechanics. **10a must land before 10b**, not as a stylistic preference but structurally: `drift_check.py`/`performance_check.py` (10b) read from `prediction_log` (10a), and `training_cycle.py` (10b) has roughly half a dozen "resolve before `training_cycle.py` is written" open questions (10a) that are pure ML/pipeline-policy decisions, not orchestration mechanics. Work through 10a's items first.

**10a further split, added 2026-08-21:** 10a itself bundles two deliverables large and risky enough to warrant separate branches/PRs rather than one — schema/serving-table plumbing (Alembic + the two new tables) versus a refactor that touches nearly every stage module in the training pipeline (`ingest` through `register.py`). **10a-i must land before 10a-ii**: the reserve-partition mechanism (10a-ii) depends on `prediction_log`/`prediction_outcomes` (10a-i) existing as its delivery and maturation path. Both must still land before 10b, per the rationale above.

**10c added 2026-08-29:** a fourth package — progressive deployment (four-state registry lifecycle, canary ramp controller, guardrail auto-rollback, continuous traffic simulator, optional compressed label clock). **Depends on 10b** and is purely additive: 10a and 10b ship and run without it. It builds and activates the shadow/canary/`require_traffic_validation` seams 10b deliberately leaves pluggable. Cold start is untouched.

#### Phase 10a-i — Data Infrastructure: Alembic + Prediction Tables *(~0.5 day)*

**What this achieves:** the durable, migration-managed schema that `training_cycle.py` (10b) and the reserve-partition mechanism (10a-ii) both read from and write to — `serving/prediction_log.py` and `serving/outcomes.py`'s write paths, backed by an Alembic-managed schema for the two tables that can't be regenerated from the CSV. None of it requires Prefect to exist.

**Deliverables:**
- **`serving/prediction_log.py`** — durable Postgres capture of every prediction (`serving/app.py`'s `/predict`/`/predict/batch`, plus `nightly_batch_predict.py`'s nightly sweep, 10b below — a different mechanism despite the similar name: `/predict/batch` is a synchronous, caller-driven API endpoint, `nightly_batch_predict.py` is a scheduled, unattended full-population sweep) — the single foundation `drift_check.py`, `performance_check.py`, and retraining (10b) all read from, in place of the Phase 9 structured JSON log stream. **Schema:** `prediction_id` (PK), `request_id`, `customerid` (nullable — what-if/prospect scoring carries none), `predicted_at`, `route` (`"single"` / `"batch"` / `"nightly_batch"`), `feature_snapshot` (JSONB — the exact row scored, not reconstructable later from `customers_crm`/`customers_raw` since neither is a time-stamped history), `model_version`, `run_id`, `probability`/`threshold`/`decision`, `contact` (batch only, nullable for single — mirrors the `decision`/`contact` separation `CLAUDE.md` § Serving already enforces at the response level), and — populated whenever a challenger was evaluated under shadow or canary — `dual_score_mode` (`"shadow"` / `"canary"` / null), `challenger_version`, `challenger_probability`, `champion_probability`; and `resolution_kind` (`"id_only"` / `"full_inline"` / `"partial_override"` / null) — *how* the row was built, deliberately asymmetric across routes rather than one unified taxonomy: `/predict/batch` reports all three (`customer_lookup.py::resolve_batch_rows` already classifies each item's shape, just doesn't expose it today — free); `/predict` (Score a Customer) only ever reports `id_only`/`full_inline`, via a new client-declared `PredictRequest.resolution_kind` field the server can't derive itself (`GET /customer/{id}` and `POST /predict` are independent, stateless calls) — detecting "fetched, then edited" specifically was cut as added client-side diff complexity for a distinction not worth tracking; a fetch-then-edited call is tagged `id_only`, and the edited values are still fully captured in `feature_snapshot` regardless. **`customerid` is deliberately not the primary key, and is expected to recur.** `prediction_id` is a surrogate key precisely because the same customer can be scored more than once — a repeat demo lookup, a genuine repeat inference, or (once the retraining union below is built) the same real customer being observed again in a later cycle after `customers_crm`'s seeded nudge advanced their tenure — and none of that is a data-quality problem to prevent. This is also why `customers_crm`'s own `customerid` `PRIMARY KEY` (Phase 9) never conflicts with it: that constraint enforces *current-state* uniqueness (one row per customer, right now, the same shape a real CRM table has), while `prediction_log`/the eventual `customers_raw UNION (prediction_log ⋈ prediction_outcomes)` training feed is a *historical/event* log, where the identical customerid recurring across occasions is the expected, correct shape of longitudinal data — not two rows competing for one key. **`request_id` carries no uniqueness constraint either, deliberately.** `prediction_log` is an append-only event log, the same shape as a Kafka topic materialized to a table, not a state table — a client retry (a network blip, a double-click) hitting `/predict` twice with the same `request_id` is expected to produce two rows, not an error or a rejected write. Downstream reads are already written to tolerate this without deduplicating on write: `performance_check.py`'s join contract takes the *most recent* prediction before a given date, so an extra retried row is simply never the one selected, never double-counted. Real duplicate-contact prevention (never messaging the same customer twice off a duplicated scoring event) belongs one layer up, in campaign/contact orchestration — not in the log. **Indexes:** a B-tree on `predicted_at` (for `drift_check.py`'s daily `WHERE predicted_at > now() - interval '24 hours'` scan, 10b) and a B-tree on `customerid` (for the `performance_check.py`/reserve-mechanism join, 10a-ii/10b, which looks up a specific customer's most recent prediction before a given date) — named explicitly in the Alembic migration rather than left implicit, since without them both queries degrade from an index lookup to a full sequential scan the moment the table has any real row count, and that failure mode wouldn't surface until 10b's flows are already running against a non-trivial table. **Write path:** a pure `build_log_rows()` zips the raw feature dict(s) against the scored output into row(s) — one function for both routes, since `score_request()` already treats a single prediction as a batch of one; the actual write happens via FastAPI `BackgroundTasks` in both `/predict` and `/predict/batch` handlers, firing *after* the response is sent so a Postgres hiccup never adds latency to, or fails, a prediction the model already produced — wrapped in try/except, log-and-swallow on failure, the same fail-open discipline `resolve_champion_model` already uses for a refresh failure. **The swallow increments a counter, `prediction_log_write_failures_total`, not just a log line.** A prediction still returns to the caller either way — that's the point of failing open — but a sustained Postgres outage would otherwise leave a gap in `prediction_log` with no signal anywhere that it happened, discoverable only weeks later when `drift_check.py`/`performance_check.py` (10b) turn up missing rows for no apparent reason. The counter is scraped and dashboarded starting Phase 13, alongside the rest of this project's Prometheus/Grafana work — cheap to add now, while the write path is being built, rather than bolted on later. **This touches Phase 9's `serving/app.py` again, deliberately not during Phase 9 itself** — the table and its write calls are both Phase 10a-i scope; building them earlier, on the pre-Alembic `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` convention, would create the exact local/RDS divergence the Alembic-adoption note below exists to prevent. **Config:** `prediction_log.enabled` (`configs/serving/api.yaml`), off in the `TestClient` suite the same way `auth.enabled`/`shadow.enabled` already are, so tests never need a live Postgres just to exercise `/predict`. **Why a table and not the log stream `drift_check.py` originally read:** log retention (local Docker stdout, or CloudWatch once deployed) isn't designed to durably hold data for as long as a churn label takes to mature — weeks to months, per the monthly-cadence note in 10b — so by the time an outcome matures, the log entry it needs to join against may already be gone, especially cost-consciously (extending CloudWatch retention costs money). A durable, indexed, joinable table is a requirement of the `performance_check.py` join (10b), not a nicety. **⚠ Known limitation, not solved here: `feature_snapshot` retains full customer PII (gender, dependents, ...) indefinitely, with no retention or purge policy.** A production system scoring real customers would enforce a retention window — commonly ~2 years for this kind of data — after which a scheduled job would purge or anonymize `feature_snapshot` (e.g. null it out past the window while keeping `prediction_id`/`model_version`/`probability` intact for historical drift/performance metrics, which don't need the raw features to stay computable). This project scores a static, publicly-available research dataset with no real customers behind it, so building that purge job is out of scope — recorded as `ANALYSIS.md` §9 item 17, the same "honest boundary" discipline this project already applies to its other documented limitations.
- **`prediction_outcomes` is this project's first non-regenerable table, which makes this the phase that adopts Alembic — moved here from Phase 8, and deliberately *not* deferred to Phase 12's RDS.** Every table before it is a materialised cache of the static raw CSV, recoverable by `dvc repro --force`; this one accrues matured labels over months and nothing rebuilds it. That is exactly the criterion migrations exist for — *state you cannot regenerate* — and it is met three phases before RDS, on local Docker Postgres, with no AWS involved. Adopting it here also means there is a real second schema version to record rather than a speculative one: `sql/schema/001_create_raw.sql` becomes migration `001`.
  > **✅ RESOLVED — the migration count above (`001`, `002`, ...) was stale the moment `customers_crm` (Phase 9) existed; the real sequence and ordering are settled here.** This paragraph originally assumed exactly two pre-Alembic tables (`customers_raw`, then `prediction_outcomes` as `002`) — but `customers_crm` (a Phase 9 addition, simulating a live CRM source for the Score a Customer tab and `/predict/batch`'s ID resolution, built on the same `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` convention) lands before this phase does, and `prediction_log` (above) is a second new table alongside `prediction_outcomes`. The actual migration sequence is **`customers_raw → customers_crm → prediction_log → prediction_outcomes`** — `prediction_log` before `prediction_outcomes` because it is the table this phase actually wires a write path for (`serving/app.py`), while `prediction_outcomes` has no write path yet, and because a future foreign key from `prediction_outcomes` back to `prediction_log` (e.g. `prediction_outcomes.prediction_id REFERENCES prediction_log`) would require `prediction_log` to exist first regardless — nothing points the other way. **`ingest.py` and `crm_data.py` must both stop executing their raw DDL directly** in the same change — each currently runs the identical belt-and-braces pattern (`001_create_raw.sql`/`003_create_customers_crm.sql` via `conn.execute(text(ddl))`) precisely so a pre-existing Postgres instance without the `docker-entrypoint-initdb.d` mount (CI's testcontainers, Phase 12's RDS) still gets the schema — `alembic upgrade head` becomes that same belt-and-braces call instead, run once at flow/CLI start, so the schema exists regardless of environment without either module owning DDL text. **`sql/schema/001_create_raw.sql` and `sql/schema/003_create_customers_crm.sql` are deleted, not archived, in the same change** — the whole `sql/schema/` directory is mounted as `docker-entrypoint-initdb.d` (`docker-compose.yml:13`), so leaving them on disk would mean Postgres keeps auto-applying them on every fresh volume regardless of Alembic, silently duplicating (at best) or racing (at worst) Alembic's own migration for those two tables. `000_create_mlflow_db.sql`/`002_create_optuna_schema.sql` are untouched — the mount continues serving those two exactly as it does today. The whole thing lands as a standalone `chore/` PR just before 10a-ii/10b need it.
- **✅ RESOLVED — adoption mechanics: reinit local Postgres, not stamp against existing state.** No shared or staging Postgres exists yet (RDS arrives Phase 12, already scoped there to run `alembic upgrade head` against a fresh instance) — the only environment Alembic needs to reconcile with today is this project's own local Docker volume, which already has `customers_raw`/`customers_crm` created via the pre-Alembic `CREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS` convention. Rather than autogenerating a baseline migration against that live state and `alembic stamp head`-ing it in place, the adoption path is: `docker compose down -v` (drops the local Postgres volume — MLflow's backend and the Optuna schema included), bring the stack back up, run the four migrations for real against an empty database, then re-run the training cycle end-to-end (`dvc repro --force` through `register.py`) to repopulate MLflow's run history and mint a fresh `champion`.
