Simpler than reconciling live state; the cost — losing this session's MLflow run history and registered versions — is a one-time hit, not a recurring one, and nothing before this phase depends on those specific run IDs surviving. **Scope boundary — `000_create_mlflow_db.sql`/`002_create_optuna_schema.sql` stay outside this project's Alembic instance, permanently, not just for this reinit.** Both are one-time, idempotent bootstrap DDL for infrastructure this project's code doesn't own the schema of: `000` creates the empty `mlflow` sibling database that the MLflow server then migrates internally via its own bundled Alembic instance on startup (the same mechanism `mlflow db upgrade` exposes); `002` creates the empty `optuna` schema namespace that Optuna's `RDBStorage` self-manages via SQLAlchemy on first connect. Neither table set is defined by this project's `src/telco_churn` code, so this project's Alembic has no model to version them against — and `CREATE DATABASE` can't run inside the transactional block Alembic wraps each revision in regardless. They stay exactly as they are: `docker-entrypoint-initdb.d` bootstrap scripts, applied once per fresh volume, outside any migration history.
- **`serving/outcomes.py`** — the write path for `prediction_outcomes` (the Alembic-adopted table above), designed and built now even though nothing on this project's static dataset will ever call it in production — the same "real mechanism, no live traffic" discipline the plan already applies to `nightly_batch_predict.py`'s shadow scoring (10b) and the monthly gate's demonstrative scoring surface above. **Schema:** `outcome_id` (PK), `customerid`, `churned` (bool), `observed_at` (timestamptz — the real-world date the outcome became known), `recorded_at` (timestamptz, defaults to `now()` — when this row was *written*, kept distinct from `observed_at` for audit purposes), `source` (`"crm_sync"` / `"manual"` / `"synthetic_seed"` — distinguishes a real integration from the manual/synthetic seeding this project actually exercises; nothing on this project ever writes `"crm_sync"`, but the column exists so a future reader can tell a demo row from a real one). **Write path:** `build_outcome_row()` is a pure function assembling one row from `(customerid, churned, observed_at, source)`; `write_outcomes()` is the I/O boundary that inserts it via the project's engine, as `INSERT ... ON CONFLICT (customerid, observed_at, source) DO NOTHING` — a unique constraint on that triple, added by the same Alembic migration that creates the table, is what makes a re-run of the CLI safe rather than a silent duplicate. This matters specifically because the CLI has no live trigger to guard against accidental re-invocation (a typo'd argument, a re-run after an ambiguous exit) the way `prediction_log.py`'s request-scoped write does — there is no upstream caller in a position to notice and dedupe. **Unlike `prediction_log.py`'s fail-open `BackgroundTasks` write, this has no live request path to protect** — it raises normally on failure, the standard boundary-error-handling rule (`CLAUDE.md`), since nothing about a label-recording CLI needs the fail-open discipline built to keep a Postgres hiccup from touching a live `/predict` response. **Trigger:** a `__main__` CLI (`python -m telco_churn.serving.outcomes`), the sole caller of the write path — in a real deployment this is what a CRM webhook or a nightly reconciliation job would invoke; here it is called manually, for 10b's verification step (seeding `prediction_outcomes` with a matured cohort of known labels, see 10b's Verification note), and never scheduled or wired into Prefect, because nothing real exists on this dataset to trigger it. Requires its own subprocess integration test per `CLAUDE.md`'s `__main__`-CLI testing rule — that test is the proof the mechanism works, standing in for a live trigger that will never fire. **Join contract this table must satisfy for 10b's `performance_check.py`:** the join against `prediction_log` is on `customerid` plus a time condition (most recent prediction before `observed_at`), never bare `customerid`, since a customer can be scored more than once before their label matures.
- **Migration roundtrip test, `tests/integration/test_alembic_migrations.py`.** Spins up an ephemeral Postgres via `testcontainers`, the same pattern the `data.ingest`/`split`/`sql_features`/`validate` integration suites already use (`CLAUDE.md` § Testing) — no `docker compose up` prerequisite. Asserts `alembic upgrade head` creates all four tables (`customers_raw`, `customers_crm`, `prediction_log`, `prediction_outcomes`) with their expected columns, indexes (the `predicted_at`/`customerid` B-trees above), and constraints (the `prediction_outcomes` unique triple above), then `alembic downgrade base` drops them cleanly, then `alembic upgrade head` a second time reproduces the same schema — the standard up/down/up roundtrip that catches a broken or missing `downgrade()` before it ships. Nothing else in this phase exercises the migration mechanics themselves: `prediction_log.py`/`outcomes.py`'s own tests run against an already-`upgrade head`-ed database and only ever prove the tables' *contents* behave correctly, not that the migrations that created them are reversible. Every migration this phase writes gets a real `downgrade()`, not a `pass` stub — enforced by this test failing if one is missing.

**10a-i Verification:** `tests/integration/test_alembic_migrations.py` passes its up/down/up roundtrip against a `testcontainers`-provisioned Postgres — the actual proof the migrations are reversible, not just that the tables end up correct. `serving/prediction_log.py` has its own `TestClient`-based test asserting a row lands in the table after a `/predict` call (§ B6 of the design notes this package implements). `serving/outcomes.py` has its own subprocess integration test asserting a row lands in `prediction_outcomes` after a CLI invocation — the closest this package gets to a live trigger, since nothing else ever calls it — plus a second invocation with the same `(customerid, observed_at, source)` asserting the row count stays at one, proving the `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` dedup actually holds. `docker compose down -v` then back up, `alembic upgrade head` against the resulting empty Postgres creates all four migrated tables (`customers_raw`, `customers_crm`, `prediction_log`, `prediction_outcomes`) in that order from real DDL, not a stamp; `ingest.py`/`crm_data.py` no longer execute raw DDL. Re-running the training cycle through `register.py` afterward confirms MLflow/Optuna come back up clean from the same reinit and a `champion` is minted again.

#### Phase 10a-ii — Training Pipeline Refactor for Retraining *(~2-3 days)*

**What this achieves:** every open ML/pipeline-policy decision `training_cycle.py` (10b) needs resolved before it can be written for real, plus the per-stage code changes those decisions require — a real (not merely demonstrative) evaluation surface for the monthly retrain, built on top of 10a-i's `prediction_log`/`prediction_outcomes` tables. None of it requires Prefect to exist.

**Deliverables:**
- **The `reserve` partition** — a 6-month carve-out from `test`, split into monthly cohorts, delivered through the real serving path (`customers_crm` → `/predict` → `prediction_log` → `serving/outcomes.py` `source: "synthetic_seed"` → `prediction_outcomes`) rather than backtested. Supports exactly 2 fold-forward retrain cycles on this project's dataset before exhaustion. **`RM1` is a one-time, non-gated bootstrap, not the first of the two cycles** — corrected during 10b's design discussion after tracing through what a routine cycle actually trains on: `run_train_step` runs *before* `merge_reserve_cohort` in the task order (below), so the very first candidate any `training_cycle.py` invocation could train would see zero matured reserve data — identical to what the current champion already trained on, and with `pinned_params` plus `random_state=42` making it a near-exact refit, there is nothing for the gate to meaningfully compare. `RM1` still needs its own `scripts/seed_reserve_cohort.py` run beforehand (served and matured by V1, same as every other reserve month), but folding it into `training_pool` is not a separate manual step — a new precondition task, `ensure_prior_reserve_cohorts_folded_in` (see the task-order bullet below), runs at the start of `training_cycle.py`'s own first real invocation (`evaluation_month=2`) and folds in every reserve month strictly before the one this cycle evaluates against, `RM1` included. It is idempotent (reuses `merge_reserve_cohort`'s own guard), so every later cycle's call to it over already-folded months is a cheap no-op — there is no separate `if v1` branch, just the same check running every time. `training_cycle.py`'s two real gated cycles are then `evaluation_month=2` (trains on `dev ∪ RM1`, evaluates on `RM2`) and `evaluation_month=3` (trains on `dev ∪ RM1 ∪ RM2`, evaluates on `RM3`) — the same shape a real deployment's walk-forward retrain takes: serve for a stretch, retrain once enough of it matures, evaluate on the next still-unseen slice, fold that slice in, repeat. Reserve months 4–6 stay defined in the manifest but unexercised — 3 of the 6 months is what this project's two-cycle demo actually needs, not a resourcing shortfall.
- **The two-gate split** — `evaluate.py`'s existing sealed-test comparative regime stays the binding gate only for a rare, genuinely-new-model-generation cycle (manual trigger, or a `COMMITTED_FEATURES` mismatch against the champion's `training_manifest.json`); a new module, **`pipelines/performance_check.py`**, is the binding gate for the routine, reserve-driven cycles. The two never run in the same cycle — a rare trigger arriving mid-comparison-window queues rather than running concurrently. **Drift alarms are deliberately not listed as an `evaluate.py` trigger here — see 10b's `training_cycle.py` bullet for why they dispatch to `performance_check.py` instead**, a dispatch-logic decision that belongs with the module that will actually implement it, not restated as a second, competing definition here.
- **`models/sealed_test.py`** — new, plain (non-`__main__`-bearing) module. `_load_sealed_test_partition` (renamed from `_load_test_partition`), `load_test_features`, `load_test_customer_ids`, `load_test_segment_lookup`, every `sealed_test_*` metric function, `comparative_deltas`, `build_gate_inputs`, and `sealed_test_promotion_decision` move here out of `evaluate.py`, which keeps only `load_incumbent_proba`/`resolve_incumbent_summary`/`resolve_evaluation_champion` (rare-regime-only) and becomes a thin `__main__` wrapper over the shared module. Required by `CLAUDE.md`'s no-cross-import-from-a-`__main__`-bearing-module rule, since `performance_check.py` needs the same scoring/gate logic.
- **Cold start is a variant of the rare/`evaluate.py` path, not a third path** — no code footprint of its own beyond `require_review` forcing (below).
- **`data/split.py`** — new `reserve_ids()` accessor (fixed seeded customerid → cohort-month assignment, its own independent random-state stream, never sharing `(dev, test)`'s) and new `sealed_test_ids()` accessor (`test_ids()` minus reserved customerids, with a defensive assertion against a silent no-op). Both persist to a new `out:` on the `split` DVC stage, `reserve_manifest.parquet`.
- **`training_pool` — new Postgres table** (Alembic migration alongside 10a-i's four), schema = every `customers_raw` column + `churn` + nullable `reserve_month SMALLINT`. Two write paths: a one-time seed inside `ingest.py`'s own DVC stage (`reserve_month = NULL`), and a cyclical reshape function outside `dvc.yaml` entirely, called by `training_cycle.py` (10b), that joins `prediction_log ⋈ prediction_outcomes` (on customerid + "most recent prediction before `observed_at`") and flattens `feature_snapshot` into `training_pool`'s flat schema — raw fields only, no engineered features and no prior-model output columns carried through. `sql/features/*.sql`/`features/build.py` are repointed at `training_pool` once (`FROM customers_raw` → `FROM training_pool`), replacing an earlier, rejected per-source-view design. Fold-forward is a query-time filter on `reserve_month`, not a write-time label. **⚠ Real gap found during 10b's design discussion, not yet fixed here — the cyclical reshape write has no idempotency guard.** Checked the schema directly: `training_pool` carries a surrogate `training_pool_id` PK with no unique constraint on `(customerid, reserve_month)` at all — the table comment explains why bare `customerid` can't be unique (a customer legitimately recurs across reserve months, by design), but nothing stops the *same* `(customerid, reserve_month)` pair from being written twice. `write_training_pool_cohort` (`data/training_pool.py`) is a pure `INSERT ... append`, unlike `outcomes.py`'s write path, which has an `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` guard on its own unique triple for exactly this reason. If the reshape ever runs twice for the same `reserve_month` — a human re-triggering a cycle by mistake, or a Prefect task retry (10b) — every customer in that cohort is silently duplicated in `training_pool`, quietly corrupting the training data for every *future* cycle that folds that month forward (double-weighting those rows, with no error and no warning). **Fix:** `write_training_pool_cohort` (or its caller) should check `SELECT 1 FROM training_pool WHERE reserve_month = :n LIMIT 1` before writing and skip (log, no-op) if that reserve month is already present — same discipline `register.py`'s `_flip_and_confirm`/`rollback_champion` alias-genericity gap above is flagged with: real, found, not yet patched.
- **`data/validate.py`/`data/checks.py`** — reused unchanged (with a cohort-sized `min_rows` override) on each cycle's reshaped delta, called from the new cyclical flow rather than the DVC `validate` stage.
- **`models/train/` — a 4-field data-assembly bundle** (`X_train, y_train, customer_ids, raw_partition`), queried once per cycle from `training_pool`, plus a new **pinned-hyperparameters mode** on `run_tuning_step` (`pinned_params` parameter, no config flag). `run_feature_audit_step`/`run_tuning_step`/`run_model_logging_step` and `common.py`'s shared helpers are renamed `X_dev`/`y_dev` → `X_train`/`y_train` (cold-start call sites unaffected; `candidates.py`/`comparison.py`/`feature_selection.py`'s CV wrapper deliberately excluded, dev-only forever). Pin for the two reserve-driven cycles; real search reserved for a manual trigger, drift alarm, or feature/schema change — the same decision as the gate dispatch above, not a second one.
- **`calibrate.py`/`threshold.py`** — both gain an optional override (`X_train`/`y_train`/`customer_ids`, plus `raw_partition` for `threshold.py`) drawing from the same train-stage bundle, all-or-none, `None` by default so cold start is unaffected. Fixes two silent dev-only hardcodes each (calibration cross-fitting/golden-fixture/SHAP population; dev-OOF reindexing and V1/V2/V2b segment lookups). Dev-OOF probability-vector persistence (`dev_oof_predictions.parquet`) this depends on is already shipped.
- **Optuna warm-starting** — `train` populates `warm_start_params` from the champion's `tuned_hyperparameters` model-version tag (below) via `enqueue_trial` (never `add_trials`, which would reimport stale scores). Pays for Phase 8's `_study_name()` cache-key fix without reintroducing the mixed-dataset risk it fixed.
- **`error_analysis.py`** — `_features_by_customer_id` gains an optional override so reserve-cohort SHAP lookups source from the train-stage `X_train` bundle instead of silently returning each customer's original frozen `customers_raw` snapshot. Runs every cycle now, not just rare ones, since `register.py` requires an `error_analysis_run_id` per version regardless of which gate produced it.
- **`register.py`** — no run-plumbing changes needed (exactly one eval run per cycle regardless of gate source, so `eval_run_id` stays a single parameter). Three additions: **(1)** `tuned_hyperparameters` tag at mint time, read from `training_manifest.json`'s `tuning_summary.selected_hyperparameters` — the resolution source for both pinning and warm-starting above. **(2)** two new tags, **`gate_source`** (`"evaluate"`/`"performance_check"`/`"v1_cold_start"`) and **`gate_pr_auc_delta`** (populated only on `performance_check`), closing a legibility gap where the existing four gate-criteria tags stop being the numbers that actually decided promotion on a routine cycle. **(3)** a third `_tag_rejected` dispatch mode for a pre-seal screen failure (`calibration_slope`/`direction_sanity`/`within_ci`), tagging `rejected` immediately instead of leaving the version for the stale-`pending` reaper. **Flagged, not yet fixed here:** `_flip_and_confirm`/`rollback_champion` isn't alias-generic — a latent bug, downgraded from blocking once 10b collapsed the flip to a single `alias=champion` act call (see the act-step bullet in 10b); still worth a one-line defensive fix.
- **Frozen decisions carried in for free** — feature-selection and model-family selection no longer re-run automatically (Phase 8's retirement; `COMMITTED_FEATURES`/`COMMITTED_MODEL_FAMILY` constants), satisfied by construction for the retrain path with no new mechanism needed. `drift_check.py`'s existing top-5-feature PSI check extends to the full `COMMITTED_FEATURES` set as the feature-selection reopening trigger.
- **Threshold/guardrail policy resolutions:**
  - **`within_ci` becomes a third binding pre-seal veto**, alongside `calibration_slope`/`direction_sanity` in `_run_dev_oof_screen`'s `failures` list — never auto-re-derives the threshold, only blocks.
  - **`require_review`** — `evaluate.py`-bound cycles (rare/cold-start) are always `true`. Routine `performance_check.py`-bound cycles escalate only on a rejection or a gate-boundary pass (deciding CI edge within one bootstrap SE of its pass/fail line, reusing this project's existing 1-SE convention); a clean pass gets zero friction. Cold start additionally forces `require_review: true` unconditionally regardless of the above, since there is no `promoted` version to roll back to.
    > **📌 The two escalation triggers on a routine cycle do not carry equal weight, and this asymmetry is easy to misread from the sentence above alone.** `register.py`'s act step has a hard, already-built rule: *"a gate failure vetoes unconditionally, checked before human review, so no review verdict can override it."* On a **gate-boundary pass**, a human `rejected` verdict has real teeth — it genuinely overrides the automated pass, the same as a human veto anywhere else in this system. On a **rejection**, a human `approved` verdict has none — the gate's rejection is checked first and stands regardless of what the human submits. So the review this policy triggers on a rejection is not a chance to rescue the candidate; it exists so a human actually looks at *why* a routine cycle failed and records it, rather than a rejection happening unattended with nobody noticing — an audit-trail function, not a veto function. Reading "escalates on a rejection" without this distinction invites the wrong assumption that review can flip an automated rejection into a promotion; it specifically cannot.
  - **`costs.yaml`-only changes get a decoupled artifact trail**, not a new model version: `threshold.py` gains a re-run mode against the already-promoted champion's existing dev-OOF probabilities, logging to a fresh small MLflow run; `register.py` gains a new **`threshold_run_id`** tag pointing at it; `serving/policy_config.py::load_threshold_payload` resolves through the tag when present. Touches three already-shipped modules (`threshold.py`, `register.py`, `serving/policy_config.py`) — real 10a-ii code footprint, not policy-only.
- **Housekeeping:**
  - **Rename `v3_direction_sanity` → `direction_sanity`** (`threshold.py`, `configs/threshold/default.yaml`) — motivated by adding `within_ci` as a plainly-named sibling criterion in the same `failures` list.
  - **No-op-month registration gating** — `calibrate` runs unconditionally every cycle (cheap, DVC cache-hits); `register.py`'s mint/evaluate/register chain gates on whether `calibrate_receipt.json`'s `run_id` actually moved, preventing a predictable `rejected`-by-construction version from accumulating in the registry on a bit-identical month. Hypothetical for this project's own two retrain cycles specifically (neither is a no-op, by fold-forward construction) — becomes live only beyond them.
  - **Elbow-drift alarm routing** — Phase 8's `*_elbow_drifted` warnings must route to 10b's structured-alarm/Prefect-notification channel (specified there), never to `model_card.json` (stakeholder narrative, not an internal diagnostic destination).
- **Deferred to Phase 10b's own design pass, not resolved here** — recorded so neither is silently lost: **(1)** recalibrate vs. retrain as statistically distinct remedies (prior/label shift vs. covariate/concept drift), which this project currently has no cheaper-than-`training_cycle.py` remedy for; **(2)** `drift_check.py`'s full-feature-set PSI extension's concrete sub-details (breach threshold, interaction with the existing prevalence-check alarm, notification routing, compute cost).

**10a-ii Verification:** every open policy question above is recorded as resolved in `ANALYSIS.md` or this document before 10b's `training_cycle.py` is written; there is no separate flow to trigger here, since 10a-ii produces no scheduled behaviour of its own.

#### Phase 10b — Prefect Orchestration Proper *(2–3 days)*

**What this achieves:** The model retrains automatically every month and checks for data drift every day — without manual intervention. The Prefect UI provides a full audit trail of every run, including failures. **Depends on 10a-i and 10a-ii being done** — `training_cycle.py`'s open policy questions are assumed resolved (10a-ii), and `prediction_log`/`prediction_outcomes` already exist for `drift_check.py`/`performance_monitor.py` to read from (10a-i).

> **❓ OPEN QUESTIONS carried over from a 2026-09-16 planning discussion, to resolve when this phase is actually built, not now:**
> 1. **Who owns CT (Continuous Training) — Prefect, or a GitHub-Actions-native flow?** This phase's default design has Prefect own the whole `training_cycle.py` chain, scheduling itself independently of GitHub Actions (the only sanctioned crossover being Prefect optionally calling *out* to a narrow `workflow_dispatch` for a redeploy sub-step, never the reverse). Phase 11 already records a fully worked alternative — a GitHub-Actions-native `training_cycle` job using GitHub Environments' required-reviewer rule in place of Prefect's suspend/resume — as a real, undismissed option (see Phase 11's "❓ OPEN QUESTION" block). Resolve there or here, but resolve once, not independently in both places.
> 2. **Where does the Prefect worker actually run, and does that mean a third Dockerfile?** Prefect Cloud (if used) only hosts scheduling/UI/run history — it does not execute flow code. Something still has to run a worker that does the actual execution, and a Docker work pool (the more reproducible, industry-standard choice, especially since training's dependencies — LightGBM/Optuna/scikit-learn — are heavier than the lean serving images) means a dedicated training/worker Dockerfile alongside the existing `api`/`ui` ones. A Process work pool avoids a new Dockerfile but needs the project's full dependency set installed directly on whatever host runs the worker. Also decide where that worker's compute lives — the same EC2 box (competing with `api`/`ui` for its already-tight 1 GB RAM, per Phase 12a) versus separate infrastructure.
> 3. **How do training-pipeline `structlog` logs get centrally captured, and is Prefect alone sufficient?** Raised 2026-09-16 reviewing Phase 12a's CloudWatch setup: `utils/logging.py` confirms structlog is routed through Python's standard `logging` module (`structlog.stdlib.LoggerFactory`), so Prefect's own log capture (which hooks into `logging` during a flow/task run) will correctly pick it up once this phase exists — but that only lands logs in **Prefect's own UI/backend**, a different destination from CloudWatch, which through Phase 12a only ever captures `api`/`ui`/`caddy` container logs and host metrics. If training-pipeline logs need to be unified with serving logs in CloudWatch too, that needs an explicit additional step — either the Prefect worker running as a container with the `awslogs` logging driver (contingent on question 2's worker-placement decision), or a CloudWatch Logs handler (e.g. `watchtower`) added directly to `utils/logging.py` as a second handler, which would work regardless of where the process runs. Not decided; resolve alongside question 2, since the answer to "where does the worker run" bounds which of these is even available.
>
> **📌 Direction recorded 2026-09-20 on question 2 (not yet a decision — confirm when 10b is built):** training must **never share the serving box**. Phase 12a's box is now a `t3.small` (2 GB, bumped from the 1 GB the question above cites) and `mlflow` alone was OOM-killed on it during Group 9; Optuna + CV + SHAP would compete with live serving, and a training OOM could take the API down with it. **Containerising training is a good idea eventually but does not solve the real problem, which is where it runs and how it reaches RDS/MLflow** (private subnet, reachable today only via the box/SSM tunnel). Suggested order: **(1)** in 10b, get the retrain flow working from wherever is easiest (laptop, or a scheduled GitHub Actions runner if network access to RDS/MLflow can be arranged) and prove the cycle end to end; **(2)** in Phase 12+, containerise it and run it as an ephemeral in-VPC task (Fargate one-off or spot instance, paying only for the minutes it runs), adding a training-image build-and-push job to `cd.yml`. The training image would ship *code* only — the trained model still reaches serving through the registry alias flip and hot-reload, never through an image, same split as `api`/`ui`. A permanently larger box is the option to avoid. The dataset is small (~7k rows), so compute is not the hard part; network access and orchestration are.

**Deliverables:**
- Prefect 3 server added to `docker-compose.yml` (UI at `:4200`)
- `pipelines/training_cycle.py` — **named for the cycle, not for the steady state; this was `retrain.py` in earlier drafts and the rename is deliberate.** The flow is regime-general: the same steps run whether or not a `champion` exists, and this phase's own notes below depend on that — cold start forces `require_review: true` *inside this flow*, and the "three separate axes" note below states outright that there is one escalation path gated differently, "not a cold-start path and a separate retrain path." A file named `retrain.py` asserts an incumbent always exists, which those notes deny; `training_cycle` is also already this project's term of art for exactly this span (`CLAUDE.md`: *"one registration per training cycle"*). This project's own cold start happened manually in Phase 7, so the flow may never take that branch here — but a fresh Phase 12 environment or a disaster-recovery rebuild would, and no code exists yet, so the rename is free now and never again. Prose that says "the monthly retrain" stays accurate: retraining is what the steady state does, not what the file is limited to. **Ripple:** `CLAUDE.md`'s `pipelines/` structure comment still lists the flows as `(retrain, drift_check, batch_predict)` — stale on three counts already (the rename argued for here, `performance_check.py` existing since 10a-ii, and `nightly_batch_predict.py`'s own rename below) — update it in the same PR that creates this directory. **Monthly cadence (first Sunday 02:00), not weekly — chosen to match how churn actually behaves, not to make the Prefect flow convenient to test.** Churn labels take real time to mature (a cancellation, non-renewal, or extended non-payment window can take weeks to resolve), so a weekly clock would mostly refit on the same largely-unmatured outcomes rather than genuinely fresh signal; monthly is closer to what telecom/subscription retention teams typically run, usually aligned to a billing or campaign cycle. The fixed monthly cadence is a floor, not the only trigger — see the drift-triggered-retrain note below. Runs `ingest → validate → features → train → calibrate → evaluate → register`, with a Prefect suspend/resume boundary between `evaluate` and `register` for the human review step (mechanism specified in the dedicated note immediately below); promotes `challenger` to `champion` via `register.py`'s gate — the same gate, in its **comparative regime** (`ANALYSIS.md` §0): PR-AUC must improve on the incumbent champion (selection), while recall@`t*` ≥ 0.65 and Brier non-inferiority hold as vetoes. Phase 7 exercised the same gate's cold-start regime; nothing is re-specified here. As in Phase 7, `calibrate.py` performs the cycle's only registration and `register.py` mints nothing new — a pass simply flips `champion` onto the same version `challenger` already points at, logged as `model_promoted`. Re-calibrated with the **pinned method** (`calibration.method` from config — never `auto`, which would let the method flip on Brier noise between monthly cycles). Inherits the operating threshold unconditionally rather than re-deriving it — the closed-form cut is a function of costs, not of the model, and 10a-ii resolved `within_ci` as a binding pre-seal veto instead: a disagreement between the closed-form `t*` and the bootstrap CI of the empirical argmax-EV threshold blocks promotion outright, it never triggers a re-derivation. **Dummy-floor guardrail:** each cycle recomputes the prevalence / `DummyClassifier(strategy='prior')` floor on the current data and asserts the retrained model clears it by a margin — a near-dummy result signals a broken feature/label pipeline (stale join, dropped column), not a modelling regression, and blocks promotion with a structured alarm. The floor is recomputed each cycle, never hardcoded, because churn prevalence drifts. **The input-space and model-family freezes, the dev-OOF persistence fix, the Optuna warm-start mechanism, and the "rolling holdout" scoping are all 10a-ii prerequisites** (already resolved/built there) — this bullet only wires the already-decided policy into flow steps, it does not re-decide any of it.
  > **📌 Review → register handoff, concretely — this is the mechanism; whether it fires every cycle is 10a-ii's `require_review` open question, already resolved by the time this is built.** Phase 8's PR B1 built `review.py` (`run_review_step`, wrapping `gate.py::record_review`) as a standalone CLI precisely so this phase would have something to call instead of a notebook cell. Concretely: `training_cycle.py` runs the automated chain through `evaluate.py`/`error_analysis.py`, then calls `pause_flow_run` (a Prefect `Suspend`) at the point `register.py` would otherwise run, and fires a notification linking to that cycle's already-written `promotion_decision.json`/`error_analysis.json` — nothing further to render, they're files. A human resumes the flow by submitting a verdict through Prefect's typed resume input; the resume handler invokes `review.py`'s `run_review_step` with that verdict, then immediately runs `register.py`'s mint/flip/reject logic in the same resumed task. Same two scripts, same functions as the Phase 8 manual flow (`python -m telco_churn.models.review` then `python -m telco_churn.models.register`, run by hand) — only the trigger changes, from two separate terminal commands to one Prefect resume.
  > **📌 The alias flip is a distinct final step, behind a `require_traffic_validation` gate — because the monthly retrain is where the missing shadow/canary stage actually bites.** Phase 7's cold-start flip has no incumbent to shadow against; this one replaces a *serving* champion with a challenger, and in a real deployment that flip would follow a shadow or canary evaluation on live traffic, never an offline gate alone (`ANALYSIS.md` §9 #13). This dataset has no feed, so structure the flow to make the absence *legible and pluggable* rather than invisible.
  > **📌 Two deliberately separate evaluation surfaces, not two attempts at the same question — worth naming explicitly, since "two-gate split" already means something else in this plan (topic 2/3's `evaluate.py`-vs-`performance_check.py` rare/routine dispatch, an unrelated axis).** The reserve-cohort comparison (10a-ii, `performance_check.py`/`evaluate.py`'s comparative regime) **decides** whether to promote — a statistical, offline comparison where the challenger is always backtested, never live-served: it's freshly trained at the start of that cycle, so it didn't exist yet when the champion originally scored the comparison-window customers, and there is no live-routing confound to correct for (see the reserve design's "Delivery mechanism" paragraph above). This `require_traffic_validation` window is a second, later, and genuinely different check — it **confirms deployment safety** on a candidate the statistical decision has already approved, catching the class of failure a backtest structurally cannot see (training-serving skew, a preprocessing mismatch, latency, a dependency issue — nothing a backtest ever runs the real serving path to find).
  > **Why not merge them.** A canary/shadow window is deliberately small and short-lived to limit blast radius, which makes it a weaker statistical basis than the full reserve-cohort backtest, not a stronger one — using it as the primary promotion signal would make the decision noisier, not better-informed. So the decision is made where the statistical power actually is (the offline backtest), and the live window only ever confirms or blocks that already-made decision — it never re-decides it. This is the standard industry shape for this kind of system (offline evaluation decides, canary/shadow validates safety before the actual traffic-serving flip) — Uber's Michelangelo and AWS SageMaker's shadow-testing feature both follow the identical two-stage pattern, for the identical reason (training-serving skew is invisible to any offline check, no matter how rigorous). The one place this pattern doesn't hold industry-wide is ranking/recommender systems, where offline metrics are known to correlate poorly with online engagement (Netflix, YouTube) — not this project's situation, since churn PR-AUC/calibration are expected to track real outcomes well.
  > **Concretely: one `register.py` act call per cycle, `alias=champion` — the traffic-validation window is a *suspend before that call*, not a second act call.** An earlier draft had two act calls (`alias=shadow`, then `alias=champion`), but the `shadow` alias had no reader. The serving layer's shadow/canary logic resolves `@challenger` (via `resolve_challenger_model`), and `register_challenger` (mint) already points `@challenger` at the new version at the top of the cycle — so the candidate is sitting behind the alias the serving layer watches before the gate even runs. Only two aliases exist: `champion` (serves real decisions) and `challenger` (the candidate the serving layer shadow-scores / canary-routes for comparison). The window is therefore: **(1)** gate + review pass; **(2)** if `require_traffic_validation` is on, `training_cycle.py` **suspends** (`pause_flow_run`/`Suspend`, same primitive as human review, bounded timeout) — during which the serving layer shadow-scores and/or canary-routes `@challenger` per `configs/serving/api.yaml`, and `performance_monitor.py` collects the challenger-vs-champion comparison from `prediction_log`/`prediction_outcomes`; **(3)** an operator (or automation) submits a pass/fail verdict to resume; **(4)** on a pass, the one act call runs, `alias=champion` — the real and only flip. `register_challenger` still mints exactly once, so `CLAUDE.md`'s "one registration per training cycle" rule holds. **When `require_traffic_validation` is on it always runs a deterministic challenger serving-parity assertion** — the challenger scored through the real `/predict` path (reserve cohort re-served, or a `nightly_batch_predict.py` pass), its served scores asserted equal to its offline `performance_check.py` scores within tolerance, closing the champion-served / challenger-offline asymmetry the backtest structurally leaves open — and **only when `canary.enabled`** (`configs/serving/api.yaml`) additionally opens the suspend window above for the slice-vs-slice A/B. **Do not fabricate traffic to make the window appear to run** — a green light wired to always-green is a prop, the same honesty line the demonstrative promotion gate 10a-ii already names. Live traffic here is scripted (`seed_reserve_cohort.py` / `nightly_batch_predict.py`) rather than organic, and with the `outcomes.py` treatment effect (bullet below) the canary window yields a genuine — if deliberately low-power — slice-vs-slice readout (`drift_check.py`'s dual-score rollup + `performance_monitor.py`'s shared sliced-metric core produce the disagreement rate, contact-set overlap, and realized slice-vs-slice A/B on `churn_realized`; see the `performance_monitor.py` bullet below), so this seam is runnable here, not stubbed. `require_traffic_validation` still defaults **off**: the promotion decision rests on the offline backtest by design (lines 655–656), and the window is opt-in for exercising the full serving path.
  > **📌 `outcomes.py` gains a retention treatment effect — added during 10b's design discussion so the canary A/B measures signal, not sampling noise.** As built in 10a-i, `outcomes.py` matures a churn label from a fixed rule that never sees the served `contact` decision — so a canary slice's realized churn differs from the champion slice's only by noise, and slice-vs-slice comparison is inert. Fix: any prediction logged with `contact: true` has its churn probability multiplied by `(1 − cfg.outcomes.treatment_efficacy)` (default `0.30`, the 20–40% band telecom retention campaigns report), drawn under **common random numbers** keyed on `(customerid, prediction_id)` so only marginal would-be-churners flip and re-running `outcomes.py` stays idempotent. Applied to every `contact: true` row regardless of which model served it, so both slices carry it — the A/B then measures whose contact set holds more genuinely-persuadable would-be-churners, which on these churn-probability (not uplift) models reduces to the challenger's precision/recall at `t*`: a low-power online echo of the offline PR-AUC comparison, matching the confirm-don't-decide role of this window (lines 655–656). Heterogeneous, persuadable-only efficacy is the richer form if an uplift model ever enters; flat is the honest match now.
  >   - **Two label columns — offline gates must never read a post-treatment label.** `outcomes.py` persists `churn_baseline` (untreated) and `churn_realized` (after the draw). **`churn_baseline`** feeds every offline surface: `performance_check.py`/`evaluate.py` gate comparisons, `training_pool` training labels, the dummy-floor prevalence guardrail, `drift_reference.json` prevalence — training on "churn given we already intervened" would un-teach the model the risk it exists to rank. **`churn_realized`** feeds the online surfaces: the canary slice-vs-slice A/B and `performance_monitor.py`'s realized-churn / retained-revenue / saved-per-contact metrics. `performance_check.py`'s reserve-cohort backtest stays clean by reading `churn_baseline` even though the champion's serve-time `contact` choices shaped `churn_realized` on those same rows.
  >     **📍 Flagged 2026-10-19, not yet resolved: this rule names `performance_monitor.py`'s *business* metrics (realized-churn/retained-revenue/saved-per-contact) as `churn_realized` consumers but never says which label its *model-quality* metrics (realized PR-AUC/Brier/the fairness-slice core, line 950 below) should read.** The same post-treatment-bias reasoning that keeps `performance_check.py` on `churn_baseline` applies here too: scoring production PR-AUC against `churn_realized` would let a well-targeted contact policy mechanically suppress churn among exactly the customers the model ranked highest, making a genuinely good model look like it's decaying — the model's own success contaminating its own evaluation. Read `performance_monitor.py`'s PR-AUC/Brier/fairness-slice metrics from `churn_baseline`, same as the gate, and keep `churn_realized` strictly for the business/financial readings and the canary A/B. Resolve explicitly when `performance_monitor.py` is actually built — not implicit in the bullet above as currently worded.
  >   - **Documented simplification:** keeping an untreated latent label is a simulator luxury — real systems observe only `churn_realized`, and training-label feedback contamination is its own topic (`ANALYSIS.md` §9). Sidestepped by construction here; noted, not hidden.
  >   - **Ripples:** `prediction_outcomes` gains `churn_baseline` (Alembic migration, per 8fd768a) — **flag as a schema change to a 10a-i table before implementing.** New `cfg.outcomes.treatment_efficacy` / `treatment_enabled` (default `true`, seed 42). `test_outcomes*` fixtures and any golden `prediction_outcomes` rows regenerate.
  > **⚠ Latent bug in `_flip_and_confirm`'s failure path — off the hot path after the single-call collapse, but worth a one-line defensive fix while it's in view.** `_flip_and_confirm`'s `except AssertionError` branch calls `rollback_champion(registered_model_name)` unconditionally, and `rollback_champion` hardcodes `"champion"` throughout (the `promotion_status=promoted` query, the alias read/delete/set) — no alias parameter. With every act call now `alias=champion` this is correct as written; it only misbehaves if `register.alias` is ever set otherwise (a manual invocation, or a future real staging use) — a post-flip parity failure would then roll back `champion` (a no-op) and leave the other alias pointing at a bad candidate. **Fix:** `_flip_and_confirm`'s failure branch takes `alias` into account — `rollback_champion` only when `alias == "champion"`, else `client.delete_registered_model_alias(registered_model_name, alias)`.
  >
  > **⚠ `register.py` act-step points settled this pass (single champion/challenger model):**
  > - **Every regime makes exactly one act call, `alias=champion`.** Cold start (v1, disaster-recovery rebuild) has no serving incumbent and no traffic baseline, so `require_traffic_validation` is **forced off** there — the mirror of `require_review` being forced *on*. Routine and rare-new-generation cycles, when `require_traffic_validation` is on, run the challenger serving-parity assertion before that call and — if `canary.enabled` — additionally **suspend** for the canary slice-vs-slice window (both described in the "Concretely" bullet above; default **off** on this dataset). The act call itself is regime-independent; only what runs before it varies.
  > - **`_complete_promotion` (step 11) needs no `alias == "champion"` gate.** With a single champion-only act call it always runs under the right alias, so `drift_reference.json`/`model_card.json`/the `promoted` tag land exactly where `CLAUDE.md` requires. (This gate would have been mandatory under the abandoned two-call design; the collapse removes the need — as it does for `_complete_promotion`'s own hardcoded-`champion` rollback branch at `register.py:2115`, correct by construction once every act call is champion.)
  > - **Reaper carve-out.** `CLAUDE.md`'s Phase 14 pending-orphan reaper treats a `pending` version older than one training cycle as a crash artifact — but a legitimately in-flight challenger can age past that while suspended for human review *or* a traffic-validation window. Fix: never reap the version currently holding the `@challenger` alias, whatever its age — it is the live candidate, not an orphan.
  > - **The traffic-validation suspend needs a bounded timeout that writes a registry tag** — parallel to the review-suspend timeout note below. On timeout or an explicit fail verdict: unset `@challenger` (`delete_registered_model_alias`) so the serving layer stops shadow/canary-routing a rejected candidate, call `_tag_rejected` with a **new** `reason` (`"traffic_validation_timed_out"` / `"traffic_validation_failed"` — the review-suspend bullet already adds `"review_timed_out"`), and end the flow `Completed`, not `Failed`. Retry = fresh `training_cycle.py` trigger + new minted version.
  > - **Config:** add `cfg.register.require_traffic_validation` (default `false`) and `cfg.register.traffic_validation_timeout`. `_rejection_description`'s docstring says "five rejection paths" (`register.py:306`) — this pass plus the review-suspend bullet take it to seven; update the count/dispatch when those `reason` values land, the `raise ValueError` fallback (`register.py:360`) already fails loudly on a missed one.
  > **📌 The escalation *mechanism*, for the policy 10a-ii already resolved above (`require_review`: `evaluate.py`-bound cycles always escalate; routine `performance_check.py`-bound cycles escalate only on a rejection or a gate-boundary pass within one bootstrap SE).** The notebook cannot be the escalation mechanism — "a human stamps `review` via notebook 05" is a Phase 7 premise that stops holding the moment `training_cycle.py` runs unattended, on a schedule, with nobody positioned to have JupyterLab open. The natural fit is **Prefect's own human-in-the-loop primitive**: the flow calls `pause_flow_run` (or a `Suspend`) at the point `register.py` would otherwise run, which persists the run's state rather than blocking a process someone has to remember is running. **Notification channel, decided during 10b's design discussion: the Prefect UI itself, not an external service (Slack/email/PagerDuty/webhook).** A suspended flow already surfaces as "waiting for input" in the Prefect dashboard (`:4200`) with no further wiring — for a self-hosted, single-operator project with no real on-call team, adding an external notification service would be infrastructure this project doesn't have a genuine consumer for, the same "mechanism demonstration, not live signal" discipline `ANALYSIS.md` §9 already applies to the drift/performance-monitoring loops. The tradeoff is explicit and accepted: there is no push alert, so a human has to know to check the UI rather than being paged — reasonable for a demo project, not a substitute for a real paging integration in an actual production deployment. The links a reviewer needs are the artifacts already on disk — `error_analysis.json`, `promotion_decision.json` — no notebook needed to *view* them, since they are files. A reviewer resumes the run by submitting a verdict through a typed input, which invokes the same `gate.py::record_review` `models/review.py` calls today — so this is a production-hardening of open-question candidate #2 from Phase 8 (the small CLI wrapping `record_review`), not a new mechanism: that CLI becomes the resume action Prefect calls, in place of a human running it by hand. Pre-Phase-10, manual CLI review remains the only option — this mechanism has nothing to attach to before Prefect orchestrates the cycle.
  > **📌 The suspend needs a bounded timeout, and the timeout has to write a registry tag — not just end the flow — or it silently reopens the exact ambiguity `promotion_status` exists to prevent.** `CLAUDE.md`'s stale-`pending`-version reaper treats *"a `pending` version older than one training cycle"* as definitionally a crash artifact — but an unbounded human-review suspend breaks that assumption: a challenger genuinely still awaiting a slow reviewer is registry-identical to a crash artifact once it's been suspended past that same age, same tag, same lack of explanation, no way to tell them apart. So the suspend's `pause_flow_run`/`Suspend` call needs a `timeout` set comfortably under one training cycle, and letting the timeout simply fail the flow run is not sufficient by itself — that only relocates the ambiguity to a predictable moment instead of resolving it; the version is still left at bare `pending` with nothing recorded. **The timeout handler must call `register.py`'s `_tag_rejected` before the flow ends** — the same shared helper the five existing reject sites (`human_rejected`, `gate_fail`, `smoke_check_failed`, `post_flip_parity_failed`, `pre_seal_screen_failed`) already route through, adding a sixth `reason` value (e.g. `"review_timed_out"`) rather than a new dispatch mode, so a reviewer scanning the version list later sees *why* it was rejected, not just that it was. **No external escalation ladder accompanies the timeout** — consistent with the notification-channel decision immediately above (Prefect UI only, no Slack/email/PagerDuty to re-ping through), a timed-out cycle simply requires a fresh manual trigger of `training_cycle.py` to try again, the same way any other rejected candidate does. **A timeout is a terminal outcome for the cycle, same as any other rejection — no promotion, no `require_traffic_validation` shadow-staging, no `champion` flip runs afterward, and the flow ends `Completed`, not `Failed`.** `register.py`'s own act step already treats a gate failure and a human `rejected` verdict identically, so a timeout fits the same family — but the timeout handler must call `_tag_rejected` directly, the way `gate_fail`/`smoke_check_failed` already do (both pass `review_entry=None`, exactly the shape a timeout needs), rather than routing through `run_registration_step`'s normal act path, which expects a `promotion_review.json` entry to already exist — the whole premise of a timeout is that no resume payload, and therefore no such entry, ever arrived. And `Completed`, not `Failed`, because this is a working system reaching a legitimate, recorded decision ("nobody reviewed it in time, treat as declined") — not a pipeline crash; marking it `Failed` would misrepresent correct behavior as a bug.
  > **📌 The typed resume input, concretely — `PromotionReviewInput(RunInput)`, mirroring `run_review_step`'s three human-supplied fields and nothing else.** Prefect 3's `prefect.input.RunInput` is a `pydantic.BaseModel` subclass that `pause_flow_run(wait_for_input=...)` takes directly, auto-rendering a form in the Prefect UI — which is what actually makes the "Prefect UI only" notification decision above work end-to-end: the reviewer isn't just checking the UI, they submit the verdict through it. Fields: `verdict: Literal["approved", "rejected"]`; `approver: str` and `notes: str`, both `Field(min_length=1, ...)` plus a `field_validator` rejecting whitespace-only strings — the same checks `review.py`'s CLI already enforces by hand (`_require_verdict`/`_require_approver`/`_require_notes`), just re-expressed as Pydantic validation so Prefect surfaces them as inline form errors instead of a CLI exception; the CLI's own checks stay untouched as an independent, still-valid entry point. **Deliberately excludes two fields a naive schema might include:** no `reviewed_at` (stays server-side, auto-stamped at resume-handling time, never trusted to a human-typed timestamp) and no `eval_run_id` (already known from the flow's own local context, not something to ask a reviewer to transcribe and risk getting wrong). **Lives in `pipelines/training_cycle.py` itself** (or a small `pipelines/_run_inputs.py` if more flows need typed inputs later) — never in `models/review.py`, which stays orchestration-agnostic; importing Prefect into it would couple a plain, CLI-and-function-callable stage module to the orchestration layer, breaking the same separation every other stage module already holds. **The form carries dynamic context, not just blank fields to fill in:** `RunInput`'s `description` (rendered above the form in the Prefect UI) is populated per-cycle with *why* review fired — cold start, a rejection, or a gate-boundary pass within one bootstrap SE — pulled from the flow's own local context rather than left static, so a reviewer sees that immediately in the form instead of having to open MLflow first just to find out which of the three applies.
  >
  > **📌 Three separate axes are easy to conflate here — worth stating together, since the natural-sounding mistake is to assume cold start and retrain use different review paths.** They don't; only *whether* the one path fires differs.
  > 1. **DVC stage execution is regime-blind.** The nine `dvc.yaml` stages (Phase 8) run identically whether or not a `champion` currently exists — the DAG has no dependency on, and no visibility into, registry state. Cold start and a retrain-with-incumbent produce a candidate through the exact same `dvc repro` chain.
  > 2. **The promotion decision's regime-awareness lives entirely inside the automated criteria, computed in `evaluate.py`.** `gate.py::decide_promotion` evaluates cold start against absolute bars and the comparative regime against relative-improvement criteria versus the incumbent (`ANALYSIS.md` §0). This is the "automatic part" — already regime-aware today, unrelated to review.
  > 3. **The review-escalation *mechanism* is one thing, invoked under different conditions — not two different things.** The Prefect suspend → notify → resume-via-CLI path described above is identical regardless of regime. What differs by regime is only whether it fires: cold start forces `require_review = true` unconditionally (no rollback target exists yet); the comparative regime fires only on a rejection or a gate-boundary pass within one bootstrap SE of the pass/fail line (10a-ii's resolution, line 635), with a clean pass getting zero friction. There is one escalation path, gated differently — not a cold-start path and a separate retrain path.
  > **📌 Route Phase 8's `*_elbow_drifted` warnings (10a-ii decided they must go somewhere a human watches) through this flow's own structured-alarm channel** — the same Prefect-notification pattern already used for the dummy-floor guardrail and the `within_ci` disagreement above — or fold it into the monthly cycle's summary alongside those.
  > **📌 Dispatch logic: a drift-triggered cycle routes to `performance_check.py`, not `evaluate.py` — corrected here during 10b's design discussion, since 10a-ii's own "two-gate split" bullet originally grouped drift alarms with `evaluate.py`'s triggers (manual override, `COMMITTED_FEATURES` mismatch) and that grouping was wrong.** A drift alarm means the population has moved since the champion was trained — but the sealed test set is a frozen snapshot of the population *before* that move, so evaluating a drift-motivated challenger against it doesn't test the thing the retrain was triggered to fix. It can produce a systematically misleading, even inverted, signal: the stale incumbent's training distribution still matches the equally-stale test set, so it can look artificially strong precisely where the challenger — built to handle where reality has actually gone — looks weaker than it would on current data, and gets rejected for the wrong reason. `performance_check.py`'s reserve/`training_pool`-sourced comparison cohort is genuinely current data, which is exactly what a drift-triggered comparison needs. A manual trigger or a `COMMITTED_FEATURES` mismatch has no such requirement — neither implies the population moved, so the staleness problem doesn't apply to them, and the sealed test set remains the right, more statistically powerful surface for those two; `training_cycle.py`'s dispatch logic must route on trigger type accordingly, not treat all three "rare" triggers as one branch. **Shared-capacity caveat, specific to this project's static dataset:** the `reserve` partition (10a-ii) supports exactly 2 fold-forward cycles, full stop (`evaluation_month=2` and `=3` — `RM1` is a non-gated bootstrap fold-in, see the reserve-partition bullet above) — a drift-triggered cycle now draws from that same bounded pool alongside routine cycles, so how many of each this project can actually demonstrate is a real, finite number this flow's design needs to account for, not a resourcing detail to gloss over. On a real, continuously-growing customer base this constraint disappears entirely: `training_pool` keeps accumulating fresh matured data indefinitely, so a drift alarm and a routine cycle never actually compete for a fixed budget the way they do here.
  > **📌 Task/flow granularity is not uniform across regimes — a real fork 10a-ii's own refactor already forces, not a free implementation choice. Stated plainly, since it's easy to read the "Runs `ingest → validate → features → train → calibrate → evaluate → register`" line above as "Prefect replaces DVC" or "Prefect just calls `dvc repro`," and it's neither: Prefect does not replace DVC, and does not uniformly wrap it either — the split is by regime.** Cold-start and rare new-model-generation cycles still run through DVC, as one coarse Prefect `@task` shelling out to `dvc repro`/`dvc repro error_analysis` — DVC keeps owning per-stage caching/dependency tracking for the frozen, file-based dataset assembly (`ingest`→`features`), and Prefect doesn't duplicate that tracking at a second layer. Routine, reserve-driven cycles — the actual steady state once this is running for real — bypass DVC entirely for `train`/`calibrate`/`threshold`: they call the underlying step functions directly as plain in-process calls, one per Prefect `@task`, because those stages read `training_pool`, a live Postgres table that grows every cycle — DVC's whole model is content-hashed files, so it structurally cannot represent "whatever's currently in this table" the way it can a static parquet. `register.py`'s mint/act and the human-review suspend/resume were never inside `dvc.yaml` to begin with (Phase 8 already scoped registration outside the DVC DAG, since a registry alias isn't a file); Prefect just carries that pre-existing boundary through into its own task graph, it isn't a new decision. The regime-by-regime mechanics: "Runs `ingest → validate → features → train → calibrate → evaluate → register`" above describes the *steps*, not how each is invoked, and that mechanics differs by which gate module the cycle dispatches to:
  > - **Cold start (v1, or a disaster-recovery rebuild — no incumbent, no matured reserve data)** goes through the existing DVC DAG against the stable, content-hashed `datasets/processed/telco_churn_features.parquet` — one coarse Prefect `@task` shelling out to `dvc repro` (or `dvc repro error_analysis`), since DVC already owns per-stage caching and dependency tracking internally. Splitting this into one `@task` per DVC stage would duplicate that tracking at a second layer — the same "second mechanism for the same shape of problem" this project already avoids elsewhere (`drift_check.py`'s bullet below). This is the Phase 8 five-step manual cycle, Prefect-wrapped.
  > - **Rare new-generation cycles** (`COMMITTED_FEATURES` mismatch or manual trigger — an incumbent exists) do **not** use that path, even though their gate is `evaluate.py`'s sealed-test regime rather than `performance_check.py`'s. A new model generation must train on everything matured by the time it fires — `dev ∪ every matured reserve cohort` — a live `training_pool` read with nothing stable for DVC to hash, the same constraint that pushed the routine path off `dvc repro` (next bullet). Training a new architecture on the *frozen original `dev`* while the routine cycles before it used fresher fold-forward data would be backwards. So a rare cycle shares the **routine path's data assembly** — `assemble_training_bundle`, per-`@task` granularity, `ensure_prior_reserve_cohorts_folded_in` folding *all* matured months (no `evaluation_month` upper bound), `raw_partition_override` threaded to `error_analysis.py`'s dev-OOF lookup — and differs from routine only in the gate module (`evaluate.py`) and eval surface (the sealed test set). It needs no `check_evaluation_cohort_matured` (nothing reserve-side is evaluated), and a trigger firing before any reserve month matures degenerates cleanly: `assemble_training_bundle` returns `dev` only, same as cold start, no special-casing.
  >   - **Is the sealed test set still a valid eval surface for a rare cycle, given its training set grew to `dev ∪ matured reserve`?** Yes. `sealed_test_ids()` is disjoint from `dev` **and** `reserve`, so the model has never seen those rows however wide its training set got — a held-out set stays valid when the training set grows around it. And a new *generation* specifically needs a **stable comparison bar** against the incumbent: judging architectural change against a rotating reserve cohort would confound "new architecture is better" with "different eval population." Two caveats, both bounded: **(1) erosion** — each rare cycle reuses the same 1,409 rows, the exact exposure `ANALYSIS.md` §9 #13 already documents; **(2) a mild train/eval distribution gap** — training on `dev ∪ matured reserve` while evaluating on the frozen seal, negligible on this static dataset (reserve months are seeded nudges of the same 7,043 customers) but real on a genuinely growing one. The *drift*-triggered case is already carved out to `performance_check.py` (line 668) precisely because the seal is stale for it; the remaining rare triggers (manual, `COMMITTED_FEATURES`) don't imply the population moved, so the seal stays the right, more statistically powerful surface for them.
  > - **Routine, reserve-driven cycles** never touch `dvc repro` for `train`/`calibrate`/`threshold` at all — 10a-ii's bundle-passing refactor (line 626-627) queries `training_pool` directly and threads that bundle through `run_train_step`/`run_calibration_step`/`run_threshold_step`/`run_performance_check` as plain in-process function calls, because a live Postgres read has nothing stable for DVC to hash against (`data/training_pool.py`'s own docstring makes the identical argument for its write path). With no DVC layer underneath giving free per-stage visibility here, each of those function calls becomes its own Prefect `@task` — Prefect's per-task retry/logging/duration tracking is the only thing that gives a human looking at the Prefect UI any granularity into which stage failed.
  > - **Regime-independent either way:** `register.py`'s mint and the human-review suspend/resume are each their own task/primitive regardless of regime — they already sit outside `dvc.yaml`, so this isn't a new boundary, just carrying the existing one through into Prefect's task graph. **The `register.py` act step is one call, `alias=champion`, in every regime** — routine and rare-new-generation cycles add a Prefect *suspend* before it for the traffic-validation window (when `require_traffic_validation` is on); cold start runs it direct (see the `⚠` act-step bullet above).
  > - **The routine path's concrete task order, worked through during 10b's design discussion: `check_evaluation_cohort_matured (precondition) → ensure_prior_reserve_cohorts_folded_in → assemble_training_bundle → run_train_step → run_calibration_step → register_challenger (mint) → run_threshold_step → merge_reserve_cohort → run_performance_check → run_error_analysis_step → (conditional suspend/review) → register.py act`.** Mint sits right after `run_calibration_step`, matching the rare/cold-start ordering exactly — `run_threshold_step`'s own signature (`run_id`, `model_version`, ...) requires a *registered* version to run against, the same requirement `evaluate.py`/`error_analysis.py` already have, not something the bundle-passing refactor changed. **`ensure_prior_reserve_cohorts_folded_in` is new, and replaces what used to be a manual, easy-to-forget out-of-band step:** it calls `merge_reserve_cohort` for every reserve month strictly before this cycle's own (for `evaluation_month=2`, just `RM1`; for `evaluation_month=3`, `RM1` and `RM2`), relying entirely on `merge_reserve_cohort`'s own idempotency guard to make an already-folded month's call a no-op — so the exact same code path runs on the very first real cycle and every one after it, with no `if this is the first cycle` branch anywhere. This does not remove the need for `scripts/seed_reserve_cohort.py` to have already run for those earlier months (serving and maturing them is a genuine prerequisite this task cannot manufacture) — it only removes the separate manual call to fold their already-matured results into `training_pool`. `merge_reserve_cohort` (`data/training_pool.py`'s write-path-2, flagged above for its missing idempotency guard) itself has no data dependency on the training steps — it only needs `prediction_log`/`prediction_outcomes` already matured for this cycle's own `evaluation_month` (via `scripts/seed_reserve_cohort.py`, still run out-of-band beforehand — see the `scripts/run_demo_cycle.py` bullet below for how that gets sequenced) — but must complete before `run_performance_check`, which reads the `training_pool` rows it writes. **`evaluation_month` (`N`) is an explicit flow parameter, not auto-derived from system state** — legibility for a demo/portfolio project (a human sees `evaluation_month=2` as a declared parameter in the Prefect UI, not implicit state-diffing logic) outweighs the complexity of auto-detecting "which cycle is next" for a project with exactly 2 known, fixed cycles. Named `evaluation_month`, not `reserve_month` — this is the flow-level parameter, renamed during 10b's design discussion specifically to stop reusing one name for two different roles (the month a cycle evaluates against now vs. the month that becomes training data for the *next* cycle); `run_performance_check`'s own parameter renames to match, since it's the one function that receives this value straight from the flow and its whole job there is evaluation. Everything downstream of the flow that is genuinely generic over *any* reserve month — `merge_reserve_cohort`, `ensure_prior_reserve_cohorts_folded_in`, the `training_pool`/manifest column itself — keeps the `reserve_month` name; only the flow-level "which month is this cycle about" parameter changes. **`training_cycle.py` is never invoked with `evaluation_month=1`** — per the reserve-partition bullet above, `RM1`'s only role is as training data, folded in by `ensure_prior_reserve_cohorts_folded_in` during the `evaluation_month=2` cycle rather than by any invocation of its own; the flow's own two real invocations are `evaluation_month=2` and `evaluation_month=3`. **Retry policy, extended:** `merge_reserve_cohort` — and by extension `ensure_prior_reserve_cohorts_folded_in`, which is just a loop over it — joins `register_challenger` in the "not safe to blindly auto-retry" bucket until its idempotency guard is built — a naive Prefect retry could re-run the reshape for the same `reserve_month` twice, hitting the exact duplication gap flagged above.
  > - **`check_evaluation_cohort_matured` is a fail-fast precondition check, deliberately not a sensor/polling task.** Airflow-style sensors (`S3KeySensor`, `SqlSensor`) or Prefect's own event-driven Automations are the standard industry answer to "wait for upstream data to land" — but both exist to handle *genuine asynchronicity*, an external process arriving on its own unpredictable schedule. That doesn't describe this project: `scripts/seed_reserve_cohort.py` is a manually-run, synchronous script — when a human runs it, the data is immediately and deterministically ready, with no external timeline to poll against. Building real polling/backoff/timeout infrastructure here would be solving a problem this project doesn't have, the same over-engineering trap `ANALYSIS.md` §9 already names for the other mechanism-demonstration surfaces. What *is* worth building: without this check, an `evaluation_month` triggered before its seeding script ran fails deep in the graph — five tasks in, inside `run_performance_check`'s `_load_comparison_cohort`, after burning real compute on `assemble_training_bundle`/`run_train_step`/`run_calibration_step`/mint/`run_threshold_step` for nothing. `check_evaluation_cohort_matured` moves the same existing check (`prediction_outcomes` has matured rows for this `evaluation_month`) to the very first task, so a wrong or premature trigger fails immediately and cheaply instead of wastefully.
  > - **⚠ Real gap found during 10b's design discussion, not yet fixed anywhere — `assemble_training_bundle` must restrict `load_training_pool_bundle`'s population to `dev_ids() ∪ {matured reserve customerids}`, and nothing currently does.** Checked `models/train/common.py::load_training_pool_bundle` and `features/build.py::build_feature_query` directly: `training_pool`'s `reserve_month IS NULL` rows are **every one of the original 7,043 customers** (`ingest.py::seed_training_pool` seeds the full raw CSV, not just `dev` — `dev`/`test`/`sealed_test`/all-6-reserve-months are all still tagged `NULL` until a customer's own reserve cohort matures and gets a *second*, `reserve_month`-tagged row via write-path-2). `build_feature_query`'s `WHERE reserve_month IS NULL OR reserve_month IN (...)` therefore returns sealed-test and every not-yet-matured reserve month's customers too, and `load_training_pool_bundle` hands that straight back as `(X_train, y_train, ...)` with no further filtering — unlike the cold-start loader (`_load_dev_features`), which explicitly calls `partition()` and keeps only `dev_df`. `load_training_pool_bundle`'s own docstring claims its `reserve_months=None` result is "the same rows `_load_dev_features()` reads for cold start" — true only of the underlying SQL population before `partition()` narrows it, not of what the function actually returns; as written, a routine cycle would train on sealed-test and unmatured-reserve customers' original features, a direct breach of `CLAUDE.md`'s "test set touched once" invariant, just through a SQL route the existing `test_only_evaluate_binds_the_test_partition` static check has no visibility into (it only watches `data/split.py` call sites, not `training_pool`/`customer_features` reads). **Fix:** `assemble_training_bundle` (not yet built — this is squarely its job) must intersect `load_training_pool_bundle`'s returned `customerid`s against `dev_ids() ∪ {customerids the reserve manifest tags with an already-matured reserve_month}` before handing `(X_train, y_train)` to `run_train_step`, discarding every sealed-test and not-yet-matured-reserve row the raw query pulled in. This is independent of the bootstrap-vs-real-cycle renumbering above — it has to hold regardless of which `evaluation_month` is running.
  > - **`run_train_step` resolves `pinned_params` from the current champion's own `tuned_hyperparameters` tag, inside the task body — not as a separate task.** `run_tuning_step`'s `pinned_params` parameter carries "no config flag" (10a-ii), meaning `training_cycle.py` itself must resolve and supply it — the source is the incumbent champion's `tuned_hyperparameters` model-version tag (set at mint time from `training_manifest.json`'s `tuning_summary.selected_hyperparameters`), so a routine cycle refits the *same architecture the incumbent already uses* on fresher fold-forward data, never re-tuning it. No "no champion yet" edge case to guard against — a routine cycle is `performance_check.py`-gated by construction, which always compares against an incumbent, so the tag is always resolvable here. The resolution itself is a single, cheap MLflow registry read with no interesting failure mode of its own, so it stays inside `run_train_step`'s task body rather than becoming its own Prefect task — unlike `merge_reserve_cohort`, it has no distinct retry/observability story that would justify the split.
  > - **`run_threshold_step`'s pre-seal screen failure needs to call `register.py::tag_pre_seal_screen_rejected` — plumbing already built in 10a-ii, unused until 10b actually calls it.** That function's own docstring says so directly: *"Called by `training_cycle.py` (Phase 10b) after it catches `run_threshold_step`'s `RuntimeError`."* Without this, a pre-seal screen failure (`calibration_slope`/`direction_sanity`/`within_ci`) leaves the version mint already tagged `pending` with no `promotion_decision.json` yet to drive the normal reject path — indistinguishable from a crash artifact until the stale-`pending` reaper eventually sweeps it, the same ambiguity the review-timeout design (above) already closes for a different trigger. **The detection mechanism differs by regime, even though the registry action doesn't.** Routine cycles call `run_threshold_step` directly, in-process — Prefect wraps it in a plain `try`/`except RuntimeError`, catches it natively, calls `tag_pre_seal_screen_rejected`. Rare/cold-start cycles run `threshold` *inside* the `dvc repro error_analysis` subprocess (the coarse-task design above) — a subprocess failure doesn't raise a Python exception into the parent, so Prefect can't natively tell which of the bundled stages (`threshold`/`evaluate`/`error_analysis`) actually failed. That coarse task's failure handler instead parses the subprocess's captured stdout (structured JSON, via the already-planned `LOG_FORMAT=json` worker setting) for the specific event name `threshold_dev_oof_screen_failed` — present, it extracts the reason and calls `tag_pre_seal_screen_rejected`; absent, the subprocess failed for some other reason and there's nothing threshold-specific to tag. Matters for the rare path too, not just routine — mint already happened (between the two `dvc repro` calls) by the time `error_analysis` runs, so a real `pending` version exists to clean up there as well.
  > - **`merge_reserve_cohort` is a thin Prefect wrapper over `data/training_pool.py`'s write-path-2 — `build_training_pool_cohort` (reshape) then `write_training_pool_cohort` (append), nothing else — populating `training_pool` for this cycle's `evaluation_month`, passed through as `merge_reserve_cohort`'s own (unchanged, generic) `reserve_month` argument.** **`customer_features` needs no separate populate step**, and this is worth stating explicitly since it's an easy wrong assumption to reach for: both `customer_features` and `charge_per_service` (`sql/features/*.sql`) are plain, non-materialized `CREATE OR REPLACE VIEW`s over `training_pool`, computed row-by-row with no aggregation to refresh — so the instant this task's `write_training_pool_cohort` call commits, `run_performance_check`'s own `evaluation_month`-scoped `SELECT ... FROM customer_features` (`_score_candidate_on_reserve`) already sees the new rows. A materialized view or an explicit rebuild step would be solving a problem this schema doesn't have. **This task is also where the idempotency guard flagged above (`training_pool` has no uniqueness on `(customerid, reserve_month)`) has to actually land before 10b wires this in** — not as a schema change, but as a cheap precondition check inside the task itself: `SELECT 1 FROM training_pool WHERE reserve_month = :n LIMIT 1`, skip-and-log if a row is already present, before calling `build_training_pool_cohort`/`write_training_pool_cohort` at all. A DB-level unique constraint + `ON CONFLICT` (`prediction_outcomes`'s pattern) was considered and set aside here: this write path always inserts one `reserve_month`'s *entire* cohort in a single call, so "does this `reserve_month` have any row yet" is already equivalent to "was this cohort already written" — a schema constraint would enforce the same fact at a finer grain than this task ever actually needs, for a table whose surrogate PK and per-customer recurrence across reserve months make that constraint a bigger change than the gap calls for.
  > - **⚠ Design correction from 10b's discussion, not yet applied to the built code — drop `run_performance_check`'s sealed-test scoring pass (Step 0) from routine cycles entirely; the reserve comparison cohort is the only evaluation surface a routine cycle touches.** As built, `pipelines/performance_check.py` scores the candidate against the sealed test partition every routine cycle and lets that pass *own* `metrics.json`/`economics.json`, while the binding verdict is computed on the reserve cohort: `_compute_reserve_decision` computes ranking / per-scenario classification / calibration on `y_reserve`/`candidate_proba` plus `comparative_deltas` vs. the incumbent, feeds them to `decide_promotion`, then **discards them** — only the verdict dict reaches `promotion_decision.json`. `economics.json` is worse: 100% sealed-test, with **no reserve-cohort EV / business-impact / capacity check computed at all** (`_assemble_payloads` builds `ev_curves`/`capacity_flags` purely from `sealed`). So a reviewer opening either file for a routine cycle sees numbers from a population that decided nothing, and `performance_monitor.py`'s "business metrics plottable across cycles" goal (below) is dead on routine cycles — the sealed partition is frozen, so those curves only move on a rare cycle. **Why drop rather than demote to a `sealed_test_diagnostic` sub-block (the earlier proposal):** the sealed test set is Phase 7's cold-start holdout, and its job — validate the first model before it ever serves — completes at V1 promotion. On a *growing* production dataset it would simply become training data by the second cycle, and each retrain would evaluate on genuinely new arrivals (walk-forward / rolling-origin, the industry standard — `ANALYSIS.md` §9 #13). This project's static dataset can't do that, so the `reserve` partition is the manufactured substitute for that missing rolling window; scoring the leftover `test \ reserve` rows every routine cycle is **residue that would not exist in a real system** — a large-*n* read on a stale population that no decision consumes, which is worth less than the small-*n* read on current data the reserve cohort already gives. The seal stays reserved for rare / new-model-generation cycles (`evaluate.py`, unchanged), never routine ones. **What Step 0 was propping up, and where each moves:**
  >   - **`metrics.json` / `economics.json`** — become **reserve-cohort** artifacts outright, no sealed-test half. Extend `_compute_reserve_decision` to also compute decile lift and `ev_by_k`/`sealed_test_business_impact`/`capacity_budget_check` on the reserve cohort (the `sealed_test.py`/`economics.py` functions are already dataset-agnostic — the same reuse the gate metrics do), and route those through `_assemble_payloads`. `promotion_decision.json` is unchanged, already reserve-cohort. Metrics logged on the `performance_check` run under MLflow's **dataset dimension** `dataset="reserve_month_N"`, attached to the candidate's `logged_model_id` — the "(model, dataset) pair" linkage `CLAUDE.md`'s evaluation section already prescribes.
  >   - **Log the incumbent champion's reserve-cohort metrics on the same run too — attached to the *champion's* `logged_model_id`, same `dataset="reserve_month_N"`.** `_compute_reserve_decision` already derives the incumbent's PR-AUC / recall / Brier / calibration-slope on the reserve cohort internally, to form `comparative_deltas` — but today only the deltas survive; the absolute incumbent numbers are discarded, which is the "persist the evidence, not just the conclusion" rule (`CLAUDE.md`) applied backwards. Persisting them via the champion's `logged_model_id` gives a **paired (champion, candidate) measurement on the identical customer set** per reserve cohort — the apples-to-apples comparison a registry column structurally can't provide (adjacent reserve months are different populations).
